Italy Fines Meta &euro;805m Over Accessing Android Users' Photos with Facebook App
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
08:34:00 UTC
17:34:00 KST

Italy Fines Meta €805m Over Accessing Android Users' Photos with Facebook App (bbc.com) 14
			
		 	
				The Associated Press reports:
Italy announced an €805 million ($887 million) fine against Meta on Saturday for using photos and videos from Android users' galleries on Facebook without specific consent or adequate disclosure...
Investigators found that the feature was enabled by default and that Facebook requested gallery access through a single permission covering multiple app functions with different purposes. The authority gave Meta 60 days to disable the feature and seek separate consent for its use.  
Italy has about 20 million Android users, notes the BBC, and this fine "is among the largest an Italian authority has ever imposed on a tech giant."
The charge is related to a feature which suggests photos and videos from a user's phone gallery as ideas for posts...  According to the Italian regulator, a preceding [authorization] notice did not make clear that this would also allow Facebook to generate suggestions based on photos in their gallery. The regulator said it took into account "the high number of people affected" and the fact it is "still ongoing for those who have not updated the app and for those who have already given their consent to the system".  It also cited "Meta's lack of co-operation". 
"A Meta spokesperson told the BBC that it planned to appeal..."

Did Alice Huang's Megaspeed Bypass America's Controls on Exporting Nvidia Chip to China?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
05:34:00 UTC
14:34:00 KST

Did Alice Huang's Megaspeed Bypass America's Controls on Exporting Nvidia Chip to China? (cnn.com) 23
			
		 	
				In three years "Megaspeed International" went from from inactive Chinese gaming subsidiary "to one of Asia's biggest buyers of cutting-edge semiconductors from the US," reports CNN.
"It is incredibly hard to look at this and not see a Chinese state-sponsored network for bypassing US export control networks," trade data investigator William George told CNN. 
As Nvidia's Jensen Huang had an affair with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, "Megaspeed quietly instilled billions of dollars of Nvidia chips in data centers across Southeast Asia," CNN writes, noting scrutiny of the company from the Singapore government and US officials "concerned that the company was passing those chips on to unauthorized Chinese buyers..."
In 2022, the US began restricting sales of semiconductors to China, including Nvidia's most advanced. While the controls were meant to curb China's tech industry, they also spawned opportunities for intermediaries willing to help circumvent them.  US officials believe Megaspeed may be one of those...   [T]rade records show Megaspeed has purchased more than $4 billion in chips and equipment from Nvidia... Based on indictments and investigative reports, research institute Epoch AI estimates that anywhere between 290,000 to 1.6 million advanced US semiconductors were sent to China in violation of export controls last year... 
Singapore also initiated investigations into Megaspeed and its controllers, including Alice Huang, for potential false declarations relating to the company's server purchases and their final destinations.   In a statement, Singapore's Ministry of Home Affairs said there was insufficient evidence that Huang had committed any related offenses, but she had been barred from re-entering the country after providing inaccurate information "to downplay her role" at Megaspeed. The other investigations are ongoing.
The exact scale of Chinese chip smuggling is impossible to quantify. However, its existence has become something of an open secret in the industry, as well as an indication that US efforts to keep adversaries from securing high-stakes technology still have fundamental flaws. A May report from the short seller Culper Research claimed that despite tightening trade restrictions, Chinese end-users still account for about 20% of Nvidia chip sales via illegal diversion and intermediaries in Southeast Asia.  
CNN reminds readers that Jensen Huang accompanied US President Trump to events with Chinese leader Xi Jinping twice this year, and also lobbied for the US to relax controls on Chinese chip imports.  "The US government ultimately approved several buyers for less-advanced Nvidia chips, known as H200, while collecting a quarter of the revenue."
"However, blacklisted companies have continued to obtain sensitive semiconductors as well," they add.  And the Taiwanese government is even seeking to criminalize unauthorized exports of advanced AI chips to China, "as prosecutors there charged nine people in a scheme to sneak Nvidia chips into China through Japan, Indonesia and Hong Kong."

Disk encryption on NetBSD – achieving secrecy
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
05:29:54 UTC
14:29:54 KST

Threat models
These are two threat models for which disk encryption is the answer:
- The device is stolen or lost and its contents must remain secret.
- The device is tampered with, and all attempts at tampering must render it unusable.
Right now, we will focus on the first threat model. It is very difficult to build a tamper-proof system, and you need to worry about tamper-proofing your hardware, depending on what kind of actor is a realistic threat.
If we want to keep data on the system secret, we must first consider which data is worth protecting. On a NetBSD system, our data can be grouped into two broad categories:
- System files (open source software)
- User-modified files
Does the /bin/sh executable downloaded from the NetBSD FTP server contain any sensitive information?
On a BSD system, due to the separation of the base system and packages, many root file system directories are mostly unmodified outside of upgrades. In a properly configured system, nearly all system daemons and user applications are not going to be running as root, so won’t have permission to modify or create files in /bin.
We can reasonably assume that /bin/sh is the same binary that every other NetBSD user is running. A user may even opt to make directories containing such system files read-only.
Problems with disk encryption
The operating system needs to be able to load files to boot.[citation needed] This makes "true" full disk encryption difficult to achieve without specialized hardware, since the user needs some way to enter a password or an encryption key, and can’t do so without some form of software running on their computer. NetBSD also needs to store some amount of metadata about the encrypted partition on the disk, such as information on how to generate a secure 256-bit key from a 24 character password.
It is common for Unix systems to use shared libraries, which are loaded by programs to access system functionality. If these become unavailable, for example, by /usr/lib going missing, it becomes much harder to use the system. Familiarize yourself with the standard utilities in /rescue which don’t need working shared libraries to be useful.
The cryptographic algorithms that get standardized by the US government tend to be slow when implemented in software. This carries a performance penalty. As a result, many modern CPUs include implementations of ciphers like AES in hardware. For those that don’t, there’s alternative ciphers like Adiantum.
Which directories are sensitive?
And which are necessary for the OS to load?
On a normal NetBSD system, several directories have a high chance of being modified during system operation:
- 
/home - user-specific files
- 
/var - log files, system-wide databases (e.g. mail notifications), crash dumps…
- 
/root - configuration files relevant to the root user account
- 
/usr/pkg and /usr/local - where third-party software is installed
- 
/etc - init scripts, system-wide configuration for the base system
A simple approach might be to encrypt /home. However, this might not be enough for some users. Although the crash directory can be easily changed with
sysctl,
a crash dump contains a snapshot of system memory which might be sensitive. If any servers have been run on the system, the log files might be sensitive. Mail sent to users (e.g. about their interrupted vi sessions and associated backups) might reveal sensitive file names.
Some users opt for the
cgdroot
approach, which uses only a small unencrypted ramdisk to pivot to an encrypted file system. However, this modifies the system startup routines significantly and may make certain rescue options harder. So, it’s worth asking whether there’s an approach that is somewhere in the middle, and still uses the standard NetBSD installation and boot process. Many BSD systems use separate file system partitions for /usr and /var, and this is a standard configuration supported by the NetBSD installer.
/etc is a harder problem to solve, since it contains files necessary to boot the system. However, on a typical desktop system, many files in /etc remain untouched. Passwords in /etc are generally stored only in hash form, except for Wi-Fi access points and certain VPN configurations (although it’s easy to reconfigure wpa_supplicant_flags to use a different configuration file from the default one in /etc). Many configuration files are only loaded much later in the boot process, after /usr is already mounted, and can easily be replaced with symbolic links to files on another file system.
The installation process
The NetBSD installer has basic functionality, but gets the job done quickly. In this configuration, we’re going to install NetBSD to a small unencrypted root file system. When you start to ignore the growth that can occur in /usr and /var and /home, even a complete NetBSD installation with development tools included is tiny. 4 GB should be more than enough.
For example purposes, I am going to use a sparc64 virtual machine, but the process is basically the same for more mainstream CPU architectures like amd64.
The installer detects that we already have a partitioned disk. We’re going to pick the "e: Delete everything" option so we have a fresh disk to start with:
Next, we’re going to choose "Set sizes of NetBSD partitions". This will allow us to have a small root, and reserve a large encrypted partition:
Here, I’ve decided I want a 4096 MB (4 GB) root file system, and a 11263 MB (11 GB) partition allocated for /var. Later, we’ll make /usr and /var share a disk partition, so don’t worry about this too much. Select "Go on".
On the next screen, I’ve changed the type of the secondary partition to "cgd" (listed under "Other type"), NetBSD’s encrypted device driver. I’ve also set its mount point to "-" so we can reconfigure it later:
I tell the installer I want to do a Full Installation and it completes as normal, installing everything into the small root partition.
When we reboot and log in as root, we enter a new world… our 4 GB unencrypted root partition.
Post-installation setup (disklabel)
Since I’m on sparc64, the disk is partitioned into disklabels. wd0 is the primary drive, and the disklabel command shows that /dev/wd0d is the partition reserved for our encrypted file system:
# disklabel wd0
#        size    offset     fstype [fsize bsize cpg/sgs]
 a:   8389584         0     4.2BSD      0     0     0  # (Cyl.      0 -   8322)
 b:   2097152   8389584       swap                     # (Cyl.   8323 -  10403*)
 c:  33554432         0     unused      0     0        # (Cyl.      0 -  33288*)
 d:  23067072  10487232        cgd                     # (Cyl.  10404 -  33287)
We can go directly ahead with creating a parameters file for the cgd:
# cgdconfig -g -k argon2id -o /etc/cgd/wd0d adiantum 256
And then load the configuration and set our password:
# cgdconfig -V re-enter cgd0 /dev/wd0d
Now cgd0 appears to the system as yet another disk, but all attempts to access that disk will be automatically encrypted/decrypted by the kernel.
The next time we enter our password, we want to be able to verify that the password was correct, to avoid reading or writing invalid data.
This is done by choosing a verification method based on the contents of the encrypted partition. For flexibility’s sake, we’re going to put another disklabel inside the encrypted partition. This will allow the system to easily verify the password is correct by looking for a valid disklabel, but also gives us flexibility in our choice of file system.
# disklabel -i cgd0
This opens the interactive editor. We’re going to enter N to set a name for our disklabel (enter something like "cgdlabel"), then enter a to modify the first sub-partition.
First, it asks for a file system type. Enter ? to display a list of types:
partition>a
Filesystem type [unused]: ?
Supported file system types:
Since I’m running -current and want to test the Log File System, I’m going to enter "4.4LFS". A more common and well-tested configuration would be "4.2BSD".
Next is the start offset and partition size:
Start offset ('x' to start after partition 'x') [0c, 0s, 0M]: 0
Partition size ('$' for all remaining) [0c, 0s, 0M]: $
Since "a" is the only sub-partition at the moment, the default start of 0 is fine. "$" will use all available space on the cgd.
Finally, we’ll save our new label and quit the disklabel editor:
partition>W
Label disk [n]?y
Label written
partition>Q
Now change the verify_method in /etc/cgd/wd0d to disklabel,
and add a line in /etc/cgd/cgd.conf to create the cgd at boot time:
cgd0 /dev/wd0d /etc/cgd/wd0d
We can now access this hidden partition as /dev/cgd0a. But for it to be useful, we’ll need to create a file system on it. Once again, here I’m using the Log File System:
# newfs_lfs /dev/rcgd0a
A more normal and well-tested configuration would be FFSv2ea:
# newfs -O2ea /dev/rcgd0a
We can now mount and access the encrypted file system:
# mkdir -p /encfs
# mount /dev/cgd0a /encfs
Add a new line in /etc/fstab for our encrypted mount point:
/dev/cgd0a      /encfs  lfs     rw,noatime      1 2
Use "ffs" instead of "lfs" if you aren’t using the Log File System. If using FFS, you may want to use the options rw,log,noatime to enable journaling.
Post-installation setup (disk wedges)
On a recent amd64 machine, your system probably uses GPT and disk wedges instead of disklabels. Assuming our primary disk is ld0, we can see a list of wedges with dkctl:
# dkctl ld0 listwedges
/dev/rld0: 3 wedges:
dk0: 20e57277-ad70-408e-9c68-225270ed1500, 9267072 blocks at 64, type: ffs
dk1: abad5a2d-8411-4d36-980e-a5f2b03a1047, 4194304 blocks at 9267136, type: swap
dk2: 2c4d136a-f7ed-48f8-aae8-38afdd50c661, 53647360 blocks at 13461440, type: cgd
In this case, our partition reserved for our encrypted file system is /dev/dk2, also accessible via the UUID alias NAME=2c4d136a-f7ed-48f8-aae8-38afdd50c661.
We can go directly ahead with creating a parameters file for the cgd:
# cgdconfig -g -k argon2id -o /etc/cgd/maincgd aes-cbc 256
And then load the configuration and set our password:
# cgdconfig -V re-enter cgd0 NAME=2c4d136a-f7ed-48f8-aae8-38afdd50c661 /etc/cgd/maincgd
Now cgd0 appears to the system as yet another disk, but all attempts to access that disk will be automatically encrypted/decrypted by the kernel.
The next time we enter our password, we want to be able to verify that the password was correct, to avoid reading or writing invalid data.
This is done by choosing a verification method based on the contents of the encrypted partition. For flexibility’s sake, we’re going to put another GPT partition table inside the encrypted partition. This will allow the system to easily verify the password is correct by looking for a valid GPT table, but also gives us flexibility in our choice of file system.
# gpt create cgd0
Now change the verify_method in /etc/cgd/maincgd to gpt,
and add a line in /etc/cgd/cgd.conf to create the cgd at boot time:
cgd0 NAME=2c4d136a-f7ed-48f8-aae8-38afdd50c661 /etc/cgd/maincgd
For now, we’ll only create one partition inside the new sub-table. I’m running -current and want to test the Log File System, so I’ll use the "lfs" partition type. A more common and well-tested configuration would be "ffs".
# gpt add -l encfs -t lfs cgd0
You should receive a kernel message, readable with the dmesg command, which notes that a new wedge is available, including its number. In this case, it’s dk3.
For it to be useful, we’ll need to create a file system on it. Once again, here I’m using the Log File System:
# newfs_lfs /dev/rdk3
A more normal and well-tested configuration would use FFSv2ea:
# newfs -O2ea NAME=encfs
We can now mount and access the encrypted file system:
# mkdir -p /encfs
# mount NAME=encfs /encfs
Add a new line in /etc/fstab for our encrypted mount point:
NAME=encfs /encfs       lfs     rw,noatime      1 2
Use "ffs" instead of "lfs" if you aren’t using the Log File System. If using FFS, you may want to use the options rw,log,noatime to enable journaling.
Final steps (moving over the data)
These are required regardless of whether using disklabel or wedges.
We’re going to copy our important directories to the new encrypted file system:
# umount /var/shm
# cp -a /home /var /usr /encfs/
We need to create a symbolic link with ln because the /usr/lib directory contains some relative links to ../../, and it expects the lib directory to be there at the root. This is a hack:
# ln -s /lib /encfs/lib
Then, we move our important directories out of the way and replace them with links to the encrypted ones:
# /rescue/mv /home /home2
# /rescue/ln -s /encfs/home /home
# /rescue/mv /var /var2
# /rescue/ln -s /encfs/var /var
# /rescue/mv /usr /usr2
# /rescue/ln -s /encfs/usr /usr
Finally, we need to add a line to /etc/rc.conf to tell it to mount the encrypted partition early, so that /usr and /var are available during the init process:
# echo 'critical_filesystems_local="/encfs"' >> /etc/rc.conf
Disclaimer
After this process, the NetBSD installer is not aware of the encrypted partition existing during upgrades. Online upgrades must be used, rather than offline upgrades where the installer is booted from removable media. This may be done with a tool like sysupgrade.
This guide was 100% written by one human and might contain human errors.

Kiri – The Modern Container Runtime

New0.2.1 Mac · Windows · Linux
The modern container runtime.
8× faster than Docker.
Kiri runs Docker containers with one daemon. The docker CLI, Compose and the Docker API work as they do today.
The 8×: docker run takes 22 ms on Kiri and 178 ms on Docker Desktop (152 ms on Colima, 279 ms on OrbStack), every engine on 2 CPUs and 8 GB.
Free, and in beta on Mac, Windows and Linux. Something broken or annoying? Tell us with Feedback in Kiri's window, and we fix it.
Kiri's window is now in 15 languages
- 简体中文
- 繁體中文
- 日本語
- 한국어

Works with what
you already use
- docker
- docker compose
- docker buildx
- Docker Engine API
- Docker Hub
- OCI images
- ghcr.io
- Dockerfiles
While it runs
Light while it runs.
Ten containers running on one Apple silicon Mac, one runtime at a time: how much CPU and memory each one holds.
CPU and memory · 10 containers running
Sample One Apple-silicon Mac, one runtime at a time. Measured numbers replace these soon. Benchmarks →
What's in it
Everything you'd reach for Docker Desktop for.
Containers were the start. Kiri now builds and scans images, runs Kubernetes and whole Linux machines, and puts any container on the internet with one click.
- Containers and Compose Every docker command you use, Compose projects grouped in the window, and a page for each container with live logs, a shell and its files.
- Images from Docker Hub Search Docker Hub from the window and watch each layer pull. amd64 images run through Rosetta.
- Builds, with a scan docker build through BuildKit, a Builds view of every build, and a vulnerability scan of each image on its own page.
- Kubernetes kiri k8s start gives you a k3s cluster in about 20 seconds, with Pods and Services in the window. kind runs too.
- Linux machines kiri machine create dev --distro ubuntu: your user, sudo, and your Mac folders at the same paths.
- Share A public link for any container on runkiri.cloud, from the window or kiri share web. More.
- Names and HTTPS Apps open at web.kiri.local, Compose services at api.myapp.kiri.local, with HTTPS from Kiri's own narrow certificate authority.
- Light on your Mac Unused memory goes back while it runs, and the VM sleeps after a minute idle. The daemon stays around 15 MB; waking takes 0.15 s.
- Move over in one go Bring your images, volumes and containers from OrbStack or Docker Desktop, then switch docker to Kiri from the window.
Prefer the terminal?
The commands you already type.
With Kiri open, the ordinary docker command runs on Kiri, and kiri adds what Docker doesn't have: a name for every container, Kubernetes, Linux machines and a public link.
- kiri run, kiri ps work like their docker twins.
- web.kiri.localhost reaches a container by name, no port to remember.
- kiri k8s start and kiri machine create for a cluster or a Linux box.
- kiri share web puts it on the internet.
Getting started
From download to a public link in four steps.
- Step 1 Install Open the disk image and drag Kiri to Applications. It sets up docker for you.
Download for Mac
- Step 2 Pull an image Search Docker Hub in Images and press Pull, or: docker pull nginx
- Step 3 Run a container Press Run and give it a port. It starts in milliseconds. docker run -p 80:80 nginx
- Step 4 Share Press Share on the container for a public link, or: kiri share 80
Compare
Same command, three routes.
OrbStack and Docker Desktop pass every container start through five programs, each with its own socket. Kiri passes it through one daemon that calls the kernel itself. Same docker command on all three.
Five hops. One shim process stays resident for every running container.
The same five hops, and a shim per running container.
One daemon creates namespaces, cgroups and mounts itself. Nothing per container is left running. Most of this time is the docker CLI starting up. Through the Docker API, a whole container lifecycle takes 3.1 ms.
Medians from one full run on one Apple Silicon Mac, every engine limited to 2 CPUs and 8 GB, all three through the same docker CLI with Docker's default bridge networking. Stops are drawn evenly; only the totals are figures. Windows has its own race.
Full benchmarks: p99s, the Docker API path and every raw figure
How it works
One small VM. One daemon. A socket between them.
On macOS, Kiri boots a single Linux VM through Apple's Virtualization.framework, with a custom kernel and a small Rust init as PID 1. The host and guest talk over vsock. Inside, the Kiri daemon creates each container with clone3. On Linux the same daemon runs on your own kernel with no VM at all, and on Windows it runs inside WSL2.
One VM, one daemonSee the numbers
Boot once
One VM serves every container. Cold boot to ready takes 112 ms, down from 810 ms on a stock kernel.
Talk directly
Requests cross one vsock channel into the VM, not a chain of Unix sockets. A round trip takes 31 µs.
Leave nothing behind
No process stays per container. OrbStack keeps a shim for each one: 143 running containers held 1.13 GB in shims on our test Mac.
Measured on one Apple Silicon Mac. Method and raw numbers are on the benchmarks page.
The app
A native app, not a web wrapper.
AppKit and the system WebKit on the Mac, Win32 and WebView2 on Windows, so no Electron and no bundled browser. Manage containers, images, volumes, networks and Compose projects, with live stats, logs, a terminal and files for each container. Running apps open by name at myapp.kiri.local. On the Mac it brings your images and volumes over from OrbStack or Docker Desktop, and lives in the menu bar.
Download
Kiri for Mac is out.
Every number on this page was measured on it. Kiri sets up a kiri docker context and brings its own docker, Compose and buildx, so there's nothing else to install. Coming from OrbStack or Docker Desktop? Kiri brings your images and volumes over.
Then, in a new terminal:
$ docker run --rm hello-world
Same docker and docker compose you use today.

On the Mac it brings your images and volumes over from OrbStack or Docker Desktop, and lives in the menu bar.
Download
Kiri for Mac is out.
Every number on this page was measured on it. Kiri sets up a kiri docker context and brings its own docker, Compose and buildx, so there's nothing else to install. Coming from OrbStack or Docker Desktop? Kiri brings your images and volumes over.
Then, in a new terminal:
$ docker run --rm hello-world
Same docker and docker compose you use today.

Show HN: Gawain – Require type hints on all Python variables
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
04:39:25 UTC
13:39:25 KST

gawain requires type annotations on the variables, parameters, returns and class attributes of Python code, then runs a type checker, ty or pyrefly, to check that the types are correct.
Type checkers such as mypy, Pyright, ty and pyrefly infer the type of a variable that has no annotation. ruff's ANN
rules and flake8-annotations ask for annotations on functions only. gawain also asks for them on variables and
class attributes.
I built gawain only because I hate dict[str, Any] and wanted to do something about it. If your answer to
this is "but Python already infers that" or "but my IDE infers it for me", then don't use it, little padawan.
Given shop.py:
PORT = 8080
retries = 3
total = compute()
def send(to, body):
    print(f"to {to}: {body}")
class Order:
    def __init__(self, items: list[str]) -> None:
        self.items = items
gawain check shop.py prints:
[presence]
shop.py:1:1    GW001 variable 'PORT' has no type annotation
shop.py:3:1    GW001 variable 'total' has no type annotation
shop.py:6:5    GW003 function 'send' has no return type annotation
shop.py:6:10   GW002 parameter 'to' of function 'send' has no type annotation
shop.py:6:14   GW002 parameter 'body' of function 'send' has no type annotation
shop.py:12:14  GW004 attribute 'items' is not declared in the body of class 'Order'
6 errors (presence). Type check skipped until presence errors are fixed.
retries = 3 passes, because a literal has an obvious type. PORT is a constant, so it needs Final[int].
When every annotation is in place, gawain runs the type checker on the same files and prints what it reports.
With ty, the default, for
prices.py:
def total(prices: list[int]) -> int:
    return "0"[types]
prices.py:2:12  ty[invalid-return-type] Return type does not match returned value: expected `int`, found `Literal["0"]`
1 error (types).
Install gawain with the extra of the type checker you want. ty is the default checker, and the ty extra pins
it to 0.0.100:
uv tool install "gawain[ty]"
# or
pip install "gawain[ty]"
To use pyrefly instead, install the pyrefly extra, which pins pyrefly 1.4.1 and does not install ty:
uv tool install "gawain[pyrefly]"
# or
pip install "gawain[pyrefly]"
Then choose it with checker = "pyrefly" in [tool.gawain], or with --checker pyrefly.
docs/configuration.md
explains the switch.
pip install gawain without an extra installs only the gawain command. It then needs a checker on PATH, or
--no-types to run the annotation checks only.
Warning
Use gawain with care. ty is in beta, and its diagnostics may change between versions. If that is a problem
for your project, use pyrefly, or --no-types to run the annotation checks only.
gawain check src/                  # annotations, then types
gawain check src/ --no-types       # annotations only
gawain check src/ --checker pyrefly  # pyrefly instead of ty
gawain check src/ --mode strict    # an annotation on every assignment
gawain check src/ --format json    # one JSON object, for tools
gawain exits with 0 when nothing is reported, 1 when it reports an error, and 2 on a usage error or
when the type checker cannot run. The type checker's warnings are printed but do not fail the check.
GW000 marks a file gawain cannot read, and GW900 and GW901 mark a broken or unused suppression. To silence a line, name the code:
value = load()  # gawain: ignore[GW001]
docs/rules.md has the full rules, the exemptions and the suppression syntax.
The default mode is obvious. A name may go without an annotation when its value has an obvious type and the
name is never assigned again: a literal (count = 0), a container of literals (ids = [1, 2, 3]) or a
constructor call (user = User("ada")). strict asks for an annotation on every assignment:
[tool.gawain]
mode = "strict"
docs/rules.md has the exact rules of each mode.
repos:
  - repo: https://github.com/matheuzgomes/gawain
    rev: v0.3.0
    hooks:
      - id: gawain
pre-commit installs the hook from this repository's source, so building it needs Rust 1.97 or newer.
docs/configuration.md covers [tool.gawain], paths and the hook in detail.
On the CPython 3.14 standard library (1,847 files, 974,586 lines), median of 30 runs (5 for flake8-annotations):
gawain ran with --no-types. This is one benchmark on one machine (AMD Ryzen 7 3700X, WSL2).
docs/benchmarks.md has the versions and the commands to run it again.
- docs/rules.md: rules, modes, exemptions and suppression.
- docs/configuration.md: paths, output, exit codes, [tool.gawain] and pre-commit.
- docs/benchmarks.md: how the numbers above were measured.
gawain reads Python with ruff_python_parser and checks types with ty, both by Astral, or with pyrefly, by Meta.
I don't know, I just like the Arthurian tales. Gawain is a knight of the Round Table, and the mascot is the Green Knight's axe with the green girdle from Sir Gawain and the Green Knight.
MIT. See LICENSE.

Show HN: Turbo-Browser-MCP – Batch forms, DOM deltas and submit guards for MCP
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
04:09:11 UTC
13:09:11 KST

turbo-browser-mcp is a Playwright-based Model Context Protocol (MCP) server built to cut LLM token waste and multi-turn latency in browser automation. It consolidates 25-33 fine-grained browser primitives into 10 batched tools featuring delta DOM snapshots, single-turn React/Shadow DOM/iframe form prefill, direct disk file uploads (no Base64 in context), parallel tab sessions, network blocking presets, and a default human-in-the-loop Submit guard.
All figures below come from benchmarks/benchmark.mjs (npm run benchmark) running headless Chromium locally with exact OpenAI o200k_base tokenization (tiktoken 0.14.0):
Note on latency vs LLM inference turns: Eliminating 10 round-trips on a 10-field form saves both 7x in browser execution (574 ms -> 82 ms) and 10 separate LLM inference round-trips (2-5s per turn in agent loops) plus ~71,800 replayed history tokens.
Standard MCP servers charge an LLM round-trip for every snapshot, field fill, <select> choice, upload, and verification check. Each turn re-sends the growing conversation history back into the model.
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    participant Agent as LLM Agent
    participant Std as Standard MCP (Playwright / DevTools)
    participant Turbo as turbo-browser-mcp
    Note over Agent,Std: Standard MCP (12+ LLM turns, full ARIA tree + history replay each step)
    Agent->>Std: 1. snapshot() -> full ARIA (~3,568 tok)
    loop Repeats 10+ times across fields, selects, checkboxes, upload
        Agent->>Std: fill / select_option / click
        Std-->>Agent: full ARIA or confirmation re-added to history
    end
    Agent->>Std: 12. upload inline or click chooser
    Note over Agent,Turbo: turbo-browser-mcp (2 LLM turns total, delta & inline snapshot)
    Agent->>Turbo: 1. inspect_compact(mode: "forms") -> compact refs (1,135 tok)
    Agent->>Turbo: 2. smart_fill_form(fields: [...10 fields + disk PDF...], returnSnapshot: true)
    Turbo-->>Agent: Filled 10 fields + guarded before Submit + inline snapshot (811 tok)
    - 4 connection modes (--mode auto|cdp|persistent|launch):
  - auto (default): probes --cdp http://127.0.0.1:9222 first so agents can reuse an existing signed-in browser session, then falls back to launching Chromium.
  - cdp: attach directly to a running Chromium/Chrome/Edge remote debugging port.
  - persistent: launch with a reusable --user-data-dir profile across runs.
  - launch: start a clean browser session (--headless by default, or --headed).
- Auto binary discovery (src/playwright-loader.mjs): checks playwright-core cache revisions across Windows, macOS, and Linux, system installs of Google Chrome, Microsoft Edge, Brave, and Chromium, or an explicit --executable <path>.
- Per-tab isolation (tabs_batch): open tabs inside named isolatedContext sandboxes with separate cookies and storage, batch-open up to 10 concurrent URLs (open_many), activate, reload, go back/forward, resize viewport, and emulate colorScheme, media, reducedMotion, contrast, forcedColors, or custom userAgent.
- Snapshot modes: interactive, forms, article, and all.
- Cross-boundary traversal: flattens main frame DOM, child iframes (includeFrames, frameIdx), and open Shadow DOM. Injects an init script hooking Element.prototype.attachShadow (WeakMap + WeakRef/FinalizationRegistry + window.__tbmGetShadowRoot) to keep post-init closed Shadow roots inspectable and guarded, traverses <slot> assignedNodes({ flatten: true }), and supports Form-Associated Custom Elements.
- Token controls: onlyChanged: true emits only elements added or changed since the prior snapshot; query filters by regex or substring; boxes: true adds [box=x,y,w,h] only when needed; maxChars caps payload size; savePath writes full output to disk.
Prefills an entire form in one MCP call while flushing microtasks and macrotasks between controlled inputs:
- Standard & framework inputs: text, email, password, number, date, textarea, <input type="range">, checkboxes, radio groups (check, not, overwrite). Triggers React-controlled state updates via native property setters and synthetic events.
- Selects & custom widgets: <select> and <select multiple> (option / values), ARIA comboboxes, ARIA switches, contenteditable rich editors, and Gmail compose dialogs (gmail: { to, subject, body }).
- Frame & wildcard targeting: target specific iframes via frameIdx, or set all: true to populate matching controls across frames.
- Direct disk uploads: pass file or files paths directly to Playwright setInputFiles without loading binary files or Base64 strings into LLM context.
- Atomic verification: pass returnSnapshot: true to inspect the updated form state in the same tool response.
- act_fast actions: click, dblclick, hover, focus, clear, fill, type (slowly, delayMs, submit), select, check, uncheck, drag, drop (file, files, or custom MIME dropData), press (key + modifiers: Alt, Control, ControlOrMeta, Meta, Shift), scroll (dx, dy), wait (ms, state, loadState, text, textGone, url), and upload.
- Targeting options: compact [eN] ref, CSS selector, visible text, label, or viewport (x, y) coordinates, with button (left, right, middle) and frameIdx.
- batch_actions: runs an ordered list of DOM actions and nested smart_fill_form steps in one MCP call, stopping safely if blocked by the Submit guard or optionally continuing on step errors (continueOnError).
- extract_clean_text: extracts readable plain text or compact Markdown (format: "text" | "markdown") from main/article containers and iframes, stripped of script and style noise.
- evaluate_js: runs JS expressions or (el, arg) element functions in any frame (frameIdx) with JSON-serialized results and optional savePath export.
- capture_page: full-page or element-scoped screenshots (png or jpeg with quality, css or device scale, optional returnImage) and PDF generation saved to a confined savePath.
- network_session presets: turbo, no-images, no-media, and clean (unblock all), alongside custom blockResources and blockUrls patterns.
- Session & storage management: read/write/clear browser cookies, localStorage/sessionStorage, and export or reload full Playwright storageState JSON files (saveStorageStatePath, loadStorageStatePath).
- page_diagnostics: defaults to errors-only (level: "errors", statusFilter: "errors") to surface failures cleanly without flooding context, while supporting all, includeStatic, urlFilter, deep request/response inspection (reqId + part), completed downloads, and dialog auto-handling (dialogPolicy: accept/dismiss).
Run the benchmark suite yourself with Chromium and Playwright installed:
npm run benchmark
Environment & methodology: Headless Chromium via playwright-core, counting exact OpenAI o200k_base tokens via Python tiktoken (approx-bpe fallback if tiktoken is unavailable). Cumulative workflow tokens model standard chat-history replay across $N$ sequential turns: $\sum_{i=1}^{N} (N - i + 1) \cdot T_i$, plus a one-shot baseline where the first turn captures context before acting.
Measured against installed MCP JSON schemas on disk (compact parameter schemas without annotations):
When to keep Chrome DevTools MCP alongside turbo-browser-mcp: chrome-devtools-mcp provides Lighthouse audits, performance trace analysis, CSS computed style panels, and V8 heap snapshots. turbo-browser-mcp focuses on fast multi-step UI automation, forms, and scraping with minimal token overhead.
- Human-in-the-loop Submit Guard (allowSubmit=false by default):
  - smart_fill_form (stopBeforeSubmit: true), act_fast, and batch_actions inspect matching elements, W3C AccName labels (isSafeSubmitter), implicit HTML form Enter submissions, key chords (Shift+Enter, Enter+Shift), HTMLFormElement.prototype.submit/requestSubmit, and same-origin iframe contentWindow/contentDocument proxies.
  - Final actions resembling Submit, Publish, Send, Post, Apply, Confirm, or payment triggers are blocked unless allowSubmit: true is explicitly passed or --allow-submit is enabled at startup.
  - Trade-off: pre-existing closed Shadow roots on already-open CDP tabs (attached before the init script ran) or strict cross-origin sandboxed frames fall back to Playwright frame handles, coordinate actions, and keyboard input.
- Workspace Path Confinement (TURBO_BROWSER_SAVE_ROOT):
  - File outputs (savePath, saveStorageStatePath) and state inputs (loadStorageStatePath) are strictly confined to the current working directory or TURBO_BROWSER_SAVE_ROOT.
  - Lexical checks, lstatSync/readlinkSync symlink verification, and realpathSync validation block .. traversal, UNC network paths, dangling symlinks, cyclic symlinks, and Windows junctions before reading or writing.

      { "selector": "#full_name", "value": "Alex Nguyen" },

In Chrome 136+/144+, enabling chrome://inspect/#remote-debugging on the default User Data directory exposes a WebSocket in DevToolsActivePort that displays an "Allow remote debugging?" prompt whenever a new WebSocket client connects. turbo-browser-mcp provides two built-in solutions so overnight AFK multi-agent runs never hang on a prompt:
- Automatic CDP Keep-Alive Bridge Daemon (npm run bridge / --bridge, enabled automatically on DevToolsActivePort):
  - Spawns a detached background multiplexer (src/cdp-bridge.mjs on http://127.0.0.1:9224) that holds one persistent upstream WebSocket to Chrome open indefinitely and intercepts Browser.close so downstream scripts never terminate your browser.
  - You click Allow once when starting your session; afterwards, any number of MCP servers, CLI scripts, or subagents can connect and disconnect all night with 0 additional prompts.
- Zero-Prompt Dedicated Profile Launcher (npm run launch-chrome / --launch-chrome):
  - Launches Chrome with --remote-debugging-port=9222 and a dedicated --user-data-dir (~/.turbo-browser-mcp/chrome-profile), where Chrome exposes standard HTTP/WS CDP with 0 confirmation dialogs even on the very first connection.
npm test            # 407 passing tests across 86 suites (26 test files under test/*.test.mjs)
npm run doctor      # Environment check for Node, Playwright, Chromium, and CDP
npm run bridge      # Start/verify persistent CDP Keep-Alive Bridge daemon
npm run benchmark   # Local Chromium speed & exact o200k_base token benchmark
Tested automatically on GitHub Actions (ubuntu-latest) across Node 20 and Node 22.

Osint Case Studies
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
03:33:41 UTC
12:33:41 KST

These OSINT case studies start where the investigators finished. Every email address and alias in them was first printed by a court filing, a US Treasury sanctions entry or named investigative reporting, about a person who has been charged, convicted or sanctioned for cybercrime. In September 2026 we ran those identifiers through the OSINTsearch email lookup and username search, then wrote up what came back. This page indexes the series: what each post covers, the technique it teaches, and the lessons that hold across cases.
What these OSINT case studies are
Each post takes identifiers that are already public and asks two questions. How far does this address or alias travel on live platforms today? And what does that teach about investigation method? Six posts follow one case or group, from Scattered Spider to TeamPCP. Six are guides that draw on several cases to teach one technique. Together they work as OSINT investigation examples with every input shown.
Search results are presence and profile data layered on the record. A display name or location is whatever the account holder typed, and none of it replaces the record.
The method in plain language
- Start from a document that prints the identifier. An indictment, complaint, affidavit, sentencing memo, OFAC entry or named news report. Addresses that circulate online for well-known defendants but appear in no primary source are not searched.
- Copy it letter by letter. Krebs printed [email protected] in 2018 and [email protected] in 2019 for the same registration. One letter changes the result.
- Run each email through the email lookup. It checks about 175 platforms and reports registrations found, plus the profile where a platform exposes one: a GitHub handle, display name and creation date, for example.
- Run each alias through the username search. It checks about 760 platforms. Any handle the email side returned gets the same treatment.
- Put every date on the case timeline. Created before the alias existed, while it was in use, or after the name went public.
- Grade each result against the record, and log contradictions next to matches.
The case studies and guides
Khoroshev, Thomson, Coelho and Rudometov are charged, not convicted, and are presumed innocent, as is every other person here who has not been convicted.
Six lessons that hold across cases
Every example below comes from a post in the table.
1. The everyday mailbox is the one that talks
Persona addresses rarely identified anyone. The Fitzpatrick affidavit describes a Riseup address used to register on RaidForums; an email lookup found no registrations for it, consistent with that role, while the personal Gmail that Google records tie to him returned 11 registrations. In the TeamPCP case, the lookup found no registrations for the address the record ties to the BreachForums "Express" account, consistent with the forum-only use the record describes, while a Gmail Krebs tied to a Microsoft account in Thomson's name returned 24 results. Persona addresses were traced instead through what they touched, as the burner email post shows.
2. A coined handle is a fingerprint; a dictionary word is camouflage
volhvb returned 11 exact matches and no near misses. joeleoli returned 33 results, all exact matches. deadcatx3 returned 21 exact-match results, including the HackerOne profile Krebs reported. Common strings behave differently: Express, the BreachForums name Krebs ties to the group, returned 316 exact-match results and the TrickBot alias mango 439. A hit on a common word is a starting point, never an answer.
3. An email anchor beats a handle match
nerowolfe, the name of a fictional detective, returned 113 exact matches. Only one of them, the GitHub account reached through [email protected], is anchored to the public record, and it carries the NeroWolfe persona Krebs tied to Khoroshev. The same pattern holds for Grubbs, whose published Gmail resolves to a GitHub account displaying "Colton Grubbs", and for the OFAC address on Maksim Rudenskiy's entry, which resolves to a GitHub account displaying "Max Rudensky".
4. Dates sort owners from squatters
A creation date is the one profile field the owner cannot type. On the lockbitsupp alias, a Steam account held by an unrelated gamer dates from 2004, years before LockBit existed (Steam lets users change a profile URL, so the handle itself may be newer). A Codeforces account appeared five days after the indictment was unsealed and TikTok and Twitch accounts followed in December 2025, all possible squatters or impersonators. In the Joeleoli case, a GitLab profile displaying "Joel Evans" dates from September 2017, before the conspiracy he admitted, while a MyAnimeList account on the handle was created roughly 21 months after the arrest and carries the same squatter hedge. On the volhvb handle, none of the dated accounts was created after the designation.
5. The record anchors, and the search illustrates
The HackerOne profile at deadcatx3 displays "ruben thomson", exactly as Krebs reported. The Medium profile on volhvb displays the Cyrillic spelling of the name on the sanctions entry. Both mean something only because a public record had already tied the handle to the name. Good sources say so. Of his LockBit reconstruction, Krebs wrote: "Does the above timeline prove that NeroWolfe/Khoroshev is LockBitSupp? No." On a handle that appears nowhere in the record, a matching display name is a lead, not a conclusion.
6. Know which document prints identifiers
The Khoroshev indictment prints no email address at all, and the IntelBroker complaint refers to "West Email Account-1". The OFAC entry for Khoroshev prints two addresses. In the Scattered Spider cases, UK and European authorities published names, ages, towns and sentences but no alias, mailbox or handle; the US filings and journalists supplied the strings to search. Our SDN email guide shows how to find them on the list.
The ethics rules this series follows
- Public record only. Subjects are people publicly charged, convicted or sanctioned. Every identifier searched was first printed by a court, Treasury or named reporting, and every claim links to that source.
- Presumption of innocence. Charged means alleged. Persona ownership is attributed to whoever established it, such as "the NeroWolfe persona Krebs tied to him".
- No minors and no victims. Neither is identified or described.
- No unpublished handles. When a lookup surfaces a handle or display name the record never printed, the post describes it generically, for example as a profile whose display name matches the sanctioned name. Unrelated people who share a handle are never named.
- A zero result is not proof. A lookup that finds no registrations says what that lookup found, not that no account exists. A mailbox is called a persona or burner address only when the record describes it that way.
- Later accounts are hedged. Anything created after an arrest, unsealing or designation is treated as a possible squatter or impersonator.
- Some published details stay out. Phone numbers, passwords, passport and tax numbers, and relatives' details are left out even when a source prints them.
FAQ
What is an OSINT case study?
A worked example of open source investigation with its inputs shown: the identifier, the document that published it, the search run, and what came back. Here, every input is a public-record email or alias, so anyone can check the source and repeat the search.
Where do the identifiers in these case studies come from?
From indictments, complaints, affidavits, sentencing memos, OFAC sanctions entries and named reporting such as KrebsOnSecurity and BleepingComputer. The journalists and firms behind that work, including Intel 471 and OpenSanctions, deserve most of the credit.
Does a matching username or display name prove who owns an account?
No. A match shows a string is registered, and display names are self-typed. The attribution guide grades each kind of evidence, and the username verification checklist gives you a worksheet for the comparison.
Can I repeat these investigations myself?
Yes. Take an identifier from a primary source, run the email lookup first, search any handle it returns, sort the results by creation date and log results that contradict the record next to those that fit it.
Run your own case study
The method works on any lead, or on your own footprint. Run a free search on OSINTsearch for an email address or username. Results show the platform and profile details such as display names and join dates where available, and paid plans export to CSV, JSON or PDF.

50th Anniversary of 'Star Wars' Celebrated with iMax 70mm Re-Release, Special Events, New Movie (deadline.com) 31
			
		 	
				"Lucasfilm and Disney have restored the original Star Wars (1977) and will release the film in multiple formats, including Imax 70mm [starting February 19], as part of the franchise's 50th anniversary celebrations," reports Deadline.
The original film will also be displayed in its original 16:9 format on the center of Cosm's 12K+ LED Dome, in a special presentation developed with Disney, Lucasfilm, ILM, and MakeMake.  They're calling it a "Shared Reality production" where "the surrounding visual environments dynamically evolve to immerse the audience in iconic Star Wars locations and moments."
Deadline reports more 50th anniversary celebrations
In addition to the Imax 70mm screenings, the film will also be presented in Digital Imax, Digital Cinema, 70mm, 35mm, and 4DX.    On May 4th, 2027, Lucasfilm will release a new behind-the-scenes documentary, The Hero Awakens, from filmmaker R.J. Cutler on Disney+.   Star Wars events already on the calendar for 2027 include Shawn Levy's [movie]  Star Wars: Starfighter, which will be released May 28. 
There's also a new poster paying homage to the original 1977 film, reports the Disney news site Mickey Visit, plus a newly orchestrated score by John Williams that they assume "will be used on all the projects that debut this year."
On April 2-3, [Disney] will debut the new "Star Wars in Concert: The Skywalker Saga" at the Hollywood Bowl. The show will be hosted by Anthony Daniels, known for his role as C-3PO.  Special performances of John Williams' iconic film scores will feature scenes and music from all nine Skywalker Saga films. The concert is a sweeping celebration of the stories, themes, and music that have inspired audiences around the world for five decades...
They also teased that new and legacy licensees — including Hasbro, the LEGO Group, adidas, Mars, Aston Martin, Steinway, Hajime Sorayama presented by Complex, and more — will introduce 50th-anniversary collections... In the opening of the press release, Disney mentioned that as part of the announcements there will be "reimagined Disney Parks and Cruise Line offerings."
  
The article adds that the April's Star Wars Celebration at the Los Angeles Convention Center has already sold out.
There's more Star Wars news, writes long-time Slashdot reader schwit1
A light saber used by Darth Vader in the "Star Wars" movies The Empire Strikes Back and Return of the Jedi sold Thursday for nearly $5 million in an auction.   The light saber, which was used in the climactic duel in Cloud City in which Vader tells Luke Skywalker "I am your father" sold for $4.875 million at the Ultimate Star Wars Auction held by Heritage Auctions. The sale marks a world auction record for any "Star Wars" collectible, according to the auction house. 
The previous record was Heritage's $3,750,000 sale of Luke Skywalker's lightsaber and severed hand effects rig on July 15, 2026.  And they're still auctioning more Star Wars memorabilia...

====================================================================================================

My favourite modern NES games
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
02:41:25 UTC
11:41:25 KST

We are in the middle of the Second ReNESance (the first one is the one that saved the video game industry in North America). The combined decades of research and knowledge on how the venerable little NES works have produced a true cornucopia of games. We are all lucky to be here to witness it.
I am always happy to inform video game fans that the Nintendo Entertainment System is still receiving lots and lots of new third-party games.
In alphabetical order, because I refuse to rank them, here are 25 of my favourite modern NES games. Almost all can be obtained digitally, usually from the developer or publisher, most for a modest fee. Digital NES downloads are cheap! Most of these can also be obtained as physical cartridges for the true hi-fi experience on original hardware.
All Hell Unleashed
A little spooky, very pixelly. This game is a real gem.
Made by 8-bit Slasher who has been honing his skills in other horror games for the NES, All Hell Unleashed is an extensive love letter to the horror slashers of the 1980s and 1990s. There is an underlying story about an evil cult that is trying to resurrect something or other, but that’s just an excuse plot so that you can fight Jaws, Freddy, Jason, Ghostface and others. It works surprisingly well. You can select your favourite horror movie from a stage-select screen to do your platforming as you fight the forces of evil, culminating in the boss fights where the villain of each horror franchise swoops in for a mano-a-mano. The platforming feels good, and the boss fights function as set pieces showcasing the real villains we are here to see in their 8-bit glory.
Finally Jason dons a better outfit on the NES than that silly purple jumpsuit!
After each classic horror villain is defeated, it is time to take on the cult itself for one final showdown. Definitely a spine-tingling adventure!
Alwa’s Awakening
Alwa’s Awakening was originally a PC game made with pseudo-NES limitations (and in the case of the music, with actual NES limitations, as the developer used Famitracker to compose all of the music). If it doesn’t feel like a classic NES game and instead plays more like a modern indie Metroidvania, that’s because it is.
In the game, you play as Zoe, a girl who likes video games and one day awakens in the mystical land of Alwa, a place from one of her games. Your task is to guide Zoe through an adventure in Alwa where she will uncover power-ups that open up new avenues of adventure. Typical Metroidvania stuff, although a bit shorter than most games in the genre. The PC-to-NES port of this game was handled by my good friend Brad Smith of Lizard fame. He made a couple of interesting YouTube videos describing the process.
The game looks absolutely gorgeous for the NES, and its Famitracker-composed soundtrack made it through the porting process completely unscathed. There was a limited physical edition that today is only available via secondhand markets. If you can grab a copy, it’s a very high-quality production. This is some of the best stuff made for the system.
Cool boss fights too!
Anguna: Scourge of the Goblin King
Anguna is an NES game with an interesting lineage. It was originally made in 2008 for the Game Boy Advance as essentially the same game, but a bit more to the standards of the GBA. Then it was successively ported to the Nintendo DS, the Atari 2600, and finally the NES.
The Atari 2600-to-NES port was done fairly faithfully, which means that the design decisions necessary for the Atari also made it into the NES port. This means, for example, no more than two enemies per screen and always of the same type. This sort of restriction isn’t unheard of in the NES, though. The very popular NES Double Dragon port also has the same limitation. The game design makes up for it and overall results in an enjoyable experience. And isn’t that what making and playing modern NES games is all about? An interesting experience made with strict hardware limitations.
So what is Anguna? At its most basic, it’s a Zelda-like. You walk around screens, you grab new tools, your tools open new paths for exploration, you use the tools to fight monsters, with the most common tool in question being your trusty sword. You are a nameless warrior who is notably NOT called “Anguna” on a quest to defeat the goblin king. Usual bare-bones plot.
Most might consider this game to be short, but I would say it’s sleek and streamlined. It doesn’t do much, but what it does it does well.
Oh, one very important upgrade that the game got during the 2600-to-NES conversion: rockin’ enemy portraits in gorgeous 8-bit pixel art!
Blazing Rangers
- itch.io
- physical (N.B. The physical cartridge is shipped by 1st Press Games, who has had a spotty track record of fulfilling requests)
Blazing Rangers is single-screen arcade firefighting co-op action! The firefighting genre is under-explored in video games and Blazing Rangers manages to represent it admirably.
The goal is to rescue the children in each burning level and proceed to the next one. To do this you have to snake a powerful firehose that can blast both flames and baddies as you make your way to the children. You may also and often must drop your firehose and rely on your backpack with water in order to get to the further reaches of each level. There are powerups to collect, which give bonuses such as increasing the length of the hose or activating a sprinkler system that will slow the spread of fire throughout the level.
You can control either of two characters, Popo and Mimi, each with slight differences in gameplay. Popo is a bit slower but can recharge his backpack with water faster while Mimi is the other way around. Each level is timed, but you’re more likely to be overrun by flames or enemies than running out of time. The children can get singed by the fire but otherwise seem unharmed, unless you get burned carrying them, in which case they get burned too.
The game has a very catchy theme song that is also the BGM of each level.
Although it can be fun enough in single player, co-op is where the game really excels. It is very fun to coordinate with your partner as there is still only one hose in co-op. A strategy, for example, could be to have one player get the hose extension powerup that the other player can instantly use to take the hose a bit further into the flames.
Changeable Guardian Estique
Estique is a horizontal shmup with the distinct honour of being made by some of the original Compile developers, veterans of the shmup genre. This is noticeable in that it even uses some sound effects from Compile classics such as The Guardian Legend. In the game you pilot the eponymous super-robot that can transform between the bipedal mecha form that can recover damage more quickly or a sleek spaceship form that is more manoeuvrable and has a faster firing rate. Gameplay consists of carefully balancing between the two forms in the heat of battle depending on whether a more offensive or defensive approach is needed at the time.
The NES manual has a deliberately awkward English translation as a homage to the quality of writing we often encountered during the licensed era. Accordingly, the Japanese and English versions of the game are not identical, with the English version having mild censorship of the cut scenes, mimicking the restrictions that Nintendo used to impose on licensed NES games.
The game’s default difficulty is a little on the easy side, and the whole game can be completed in under an hour at the default difficulty. I get the impression that the intended difficulty is a bit higher, so you can always adjust it up for your gaming needs.
Regardless of the difficulty, the giant screen-filling boss fights are always a delight.
The Cowlitz Gamers’ 2nd Adventure
Why 2nd adventure, you might ask? Well, this game has an interesting history. The original adventure was written way back in 2015 to support the Arc of Cowlitz County charity. Within a couple of years, a sequel appeared and physical copies were made and distributed at the Portland Retro Gaming Expo. They aren’t made anymore, but if you’re exceedingly lucky, you might be able to nab a second-hand copy somewhere. Finally the game was uploaded to itch.io as a free download, with a nudge to donate to the charity.
The game is a tightly-made single-screen arcade platformer where siblings Kaylee and Justice decide to go down into the mines and see who can collect the most gold and diamonds to buy fireworks for the festivities. It can be played co-op or single-player, where the co-op is also half competitive mode to see who can pick up the most gems. The siblings have no way to directly attack any of the enemies in the mines, so it’s all up to careful jumping and dodging to clear each screen of gold in order to open the way up to the next screen.
The platforming is responsive and engaging. Very fun to play. It’s no surprise then that this game has achieved some popularity with speedrunners.
The music is a real standout and full of earworms. Several chiptune artists contributed to this game’s vibrant soundtrack. I often find myself humming the tune of the first few levels. Sometimes I even go into the options screen where the sound test lets me play all of the game’s tunes and use it as my jukebox.
The game’s colourful graphics perfectly complement the music.
Data Man
Okay, before we go on, I have to point out this game’s hilarious retro-themed trailer.
Those two kids furiously mashing the buttons always give me a laugh. That’s exactly the same look I have when I play this game.
Data Man is an arena shooter in the style of Xeno Crisis or Smash TV. Like most arena shooters, a second player can join in to fight all the baddies. Unlike most arena shooters, you don’t actually die if the baddies get to you, because what you are trying to defend isn’t yourself, but the very chips of your NES.
You see, bugs have invaded your NES. They are going for your CPU! You must defend your CPU at all costs before the bugs manage to eat their way through it. There are little bugs, big bugs, boss bugs, and they are all trying to chomp onto your CPU. You will only be momentarily stunned and lose some of your firepower if the bugs injure you. Their real target is your computer’s chips. You can blow up capacitors if need be, and their explosions can take out some bugs. That’s okay. Defend your CPU from waves and waves of bugs in order to clear the current level!
Should you fail in your mission and the bugs manage to wreck your CPU, your very NES will be unable to continue and you will be treated to a glitch fest of a game over.
I find the game rather difficult in single player and more enjoyable in co-op. If you can find a friend to play with you, I recommend doing so.
Dungeons and DoomKnights
This game has high production values. It’s an 8-bit spinoff of the AdventureQuest universe, a universe that recently obtained the distinction of being the most over-funded Kickstarter ever (their funding goal was one dollar, so each dollar added 100% more to their funding goal, haha).
It is a game that doesn’t and also does take itself seriously. There is serious gameplay here, as our hero Artix and his deadly pomeranian explore a world plagued by the undead and the infernal. There are both top-down and side view action sequences, lots of action, and lots of secrets to explore. The exploration is fun, the action is fun, and I am very glad this game exists. They crafted a world with many nooks and crannies to discover.
Should you get the physical version, you’re in for a treat. Artix Entertainment took lots of care to ensure you got a complete package. You’ll get the game, feelies, a certificate, and a copy of Pretendo Empower, (also available digitally) where you can read about all the lore and secrets of this game.
Battle on!
Garbage Pail Kids: Mad Mike and the Quest for Stale Gum
Remember the Garbage Pail Kids? The gross-out cards that were a parody of Cabbage Patch kids, created by Art Spiegelman, the same Art Spiegelman who made the poignant and award-winning Maus? Well, what if the Garbage Pail Kids branched out into one of the most popular media of their heyday, the NES?
I’ll admit, when I first watched the trailer mockumentary, I was briefly deceived that this was an actual game that was recently unearthed.
This is an NES game alright, although an actual NES copy is rather elusive. There was a run of physical NES cartridges made by iam8bit but it was sold out long ago. There is no digital distribution of the actual NES ROM although the Steam version does seem to have the actual ROM hidden in there somewhere, with Cowabunga reportedly being able to extract it. The second-hand market seems to be the only way to get a physical copy nowadays.
As to playing the game, gross-out humour and every sort of bodily excretion is of course the main attraction. It’s a very well-made exploration platformer where you can switch between four of the titular kids, each with their own disgusting abilities.
Mad Mike can fight with his axe and sword. Leaky Lindsay can fling boogers at the enemies. Luke Puke’s vomit projectiles downwards instead, sort of like Simon Belmont’s holy water, but a lot less holy. And Patty Putty is an extra-bouncy baby who jumps high and stomps down butt-first with a full diaper.
Retrotainment programmed the game, with many years of NES experience, and it shows. The game plays and controls beautifully and the level design is interesting, with close fidelity to the source material.
Gunhawk
People are still experimenting with NES peripherals. People such as Hexcellent have worked on Power Pad-based glitch art installations* . Micro Mages has given new life to 4-player adapters, and if they ever finish it, even RAD vs Dragons will breathe new life into ROB the NES Robot.
For the venerable Zapper, we have Gunhawk. The game is controlled either with gamepad to dodge and the Zapper to aim and shoot, or it can be controlled with just the gamepad alone, using the B button to shoot and holding down the A button to dodge. The intended experience does seem to be with the Zapper, as I found it more difficult to use the gamepad because you cannot aim and dodge at the same time.
We take the pilot’s seat of an experimental mecha stolen and commandeered by Nue, who has stolen the titular Gunhawk in order to escape from some vaguely evil military base. Emma, a local resistance leader, will contact us in levels with cut scenes that advance the story. You have to clear waves of enemies with various movements and attack patterns. Your shot charges automatically in a way that reminds me of the Battle Clash games for the SNES that used the Super Scope. Boss enemies also make an appearance at the end of each sector, and there are upgrades to obtain as well as taking a gamble in hacking scenes between levels (no hacking minigame, as far as I can tell, it’s just a dice roll if your hacking will succeed or fail).
Here is Y.T. showing how the game is played.
The gameplay is challenging and varied. The gamepad + zapper control scheme is effective. If you decide to get the physical version, you will get secrets in the manual that can be read with a red filter as was the style at the time.
* Which despite being, well, “(h)excellent”, I did not include in this list because it’s more of glitch art toy than a game.
GunTneR
From the madcap dreams of Langel Bookbinder (this guy) comes GunTneR, a crazy fun horizontal shmup that goes RIGHT-TO-LEFT (seriously, what kind of shmup goes in that direction? I can’t think of any other!)
In GunTneR we pilot the Rudy, a ship of the titular GunTneR class across the “13th dimension”… actually, y’know what, let’s just leave the explanation to the manual:
I crack up every time I read that, yes, including right now as I’m writing this. Bookbinder’s peculiar sense of humour shines through in this game.
And the game is fun to play too. It’s a timed mission to take a Dingle to some sort of space veterinarian or something. The sooner you get there, the healthier your Dingle will be. You have to clear out each wave of baddies, carefully balancing your ship’s shield levels with the occasional frenetic rapid-fire burst you can activate to help you clear out the current wave. There is a power-up juggle mechanic similar to Konami’s Pop’n Twinbee bells that can help you keep a power-up on the screen to be collected at a later time as may be convenient. You will receive “congrations” after clearing each boss enemy.
There is so much going on at once that it’s sometimes hard to keep up, but with some practice you can learn what needs to be dodged, what needs to be shot, and what needs to be collected.
And look! I’m a low-dimensional fan!
Haunted: Halloween Trilogy
Three times now since 2015, the Retrotainment team has regaled us with a Halloween adventure right on time, an irregular tradition for October 31st.
In the first game we take control of Donny Johnstown, elementary-school aged-kid who has to battle the undead forces of Haunted Harry over many levels of undead platforming horror in the town of Possum Hollow. In ’86, now in middle school, Tami can join the fray via co-op tag-team action. This actually introduces a sort of hidden co-op mode, as a second player holding the second gamepad can hit select at any time to tag in. This mechanic returns in ’87, along with some non-linearity to the level selection as well as some added shmup sections on a flying broom. It is the year of the Witch, after all.
The three games have an overarching story as Donny and Tami discover more of the mystery of Possum Hollow and thrice thwart the thanatological throngs and theatrics of Haunted Harry. The ’86 game grows Donny’s and Tami’s move set letting you choose an upgrade at the end of each level, and the full arsenal is available from the beginning in ’87. The polish across the three games gradually increases as Retrotainment hones their craft. They are well-designed platformers, with beautiful background art and spritework and tight, responsive controls. The three games in unison craft a perfect spooky atmosphere for any Halloween.
I gotta say, of all the methods that fiction has come up with for dealing with the undead, drop-kicking them or decapitating them with a well-placed uppercut is one of the more novel ones.
Jane Austen’s 8-bit Adventure
- itch.io
- Steam
- Nintendo store
- (Physical versions sold out, but the authors have made vague promises of another possible future run of cartridges)
It sounds like a joke,
but it’s a surprisingly well-made game.
Being a fan of Jane Austen’s work isn’t a prerequisite, but it helps to be acquainted in order to grasp some of the details and setting of this game. The gameplay is quintessential NES platforming, with good controls and an interesting central mechanic. It’s not too difficult or too long, but it’s not short either. It feels just right with just the right amount of challenge.
The story is that Jane Austen finds herself in an unfamiliar 8-bit world and characters she portrayed unfavourably in her books are her main antagonists. Using her quill to attack (which after all, is mightier than the sword) and collecting cups of tea for ammunition, she must traverse platforming levels inspired by each of her novels until she can find a way out of this world.
It all sounds rather silly but it works very well and the game is actually quite fun. Although my knowledge of Jane Austen begins and ends with Clueless and “it is a truth universally acknowledged…“, the game gave me a desire to read more of her work.
Lizard
My lizard is the lizard of tagline
Put on a lizard and go on an adventure!
My lizard is the lizard of reminiscence
Lizard for me is a very personal and emotional game. I met the author Brad Smith via this game and we have since become good friends. We play video games almost every week and chat about whatever is crossing our minds. And that feels a lot like what Lizard feels like. Lizard is a meandering, personal, somewhat aimless game. Sometimes I joke that it’s just Brad’s mad science experiment on human brains. Lizard presents you with challenges, puzzles, discovery, and frustration and makes no attempt to dictate how you should react: surprise, determination, desperation. Lizard just sits there quietly waiting to see your ensuing feelings.
My lizard is the lizard of lore
I should explain at this point what the gameplay actually is. It’s an open-world exploration platformer. Lizard dumps you in the middle of a world that gives you no objective and no explanation save what you can find on your own. You begin by wearing the Lizard of Knowledge and if you read the manual you can get some pointers that there are other lizards you may discover. There is no direction you should specifically explore, and the denizens of Lizard seem downright disinterested in you. With narrow exceptions you cannot hurt them, and they will not attack you unless you approach them. Everyone was simply minding their business until you showed up wearing a lizard.
The game actually feels oneiric because it sort of is inspired by a dream. One of Brad’s friends once awoke from a dream, distressed and proclaiming, “my lizard is not the lizard of knowledge! My lizard is not the lizard of knowledge!!“. Curious to discover what the Lizard of Knowledge might be and what other lizards there may be, Brad spent years making this game.
My lizard is the lizard of multimedia
Lizard is an NES game, but it has also been ported to many other platforms. For me, however, the full Lizard experience includes all the supplemental materials Brad has provided, most of which can be found on his website. Therein you can find source code for the game, developer blog posts, videos explaining how the game was made, cheats, and so much more. Lizard is an NES game, but it’s an NES game that is keenly self-aware that it is a modern NES game, and that people are allowed to experience it however they see fit. You can play a superficial platformer or you can engage deeply with its secrets.
MicroMages
Ah, Micro Mages… probably one of the most explosively popular modern NES games. Hey, there’s a good chance that you’ve already seen its making-of video, and if you haven’t, now is always a good time to do so:
In the world of modern NES games, Micro Mages has become an institution. The premise is simple: four mages teleport to the fortress atop Pretzel Mountain to save their beloved Princess. You can shoot, charge shoot, jump, and wall-jump. That’s your lot. Now go and fight the forces of evil!
What makes people love Micro Mages is how well it all works together in such a, well, micro package. The Morphcat duo of Nicolas Bétoux and Julius Riecke created a real masterpiece. The gameplay is engaging, just the right difficulty curve, with perfectly-designed levels that gradually ramp up challenge. The game also has the rare distinction of being a 4-player game, and it’s an absolute blast to play it that way. The four mages can push each other, which can both help and hinder gameplay depending on how you play. Will you help your fellow mage reach the top of the tower, or will you selfishly push them into the abyss and collect the treasures by yourself?
The neat thing is that dying in co-op doesn’t mean you’re out of the game. Downed mages become ghosts, and if you find the right power-up you can come back to life. In the meantime, they can help the live mages by temporarily freezing baddies with their spectral shots. All mages share a single life counter, which only goes down if there are no surviving mages left in that attempt.
It has become a sort of tradition at my household that new guests get to experience at least some Micro Mages co-op with my family on original hardware.
The game has been around for a while, so it may have already been ported to your modern console of choice, including Switch, PS4, and PS5.
Lastly, if you can’t get enough Micro Mages and you want more levels, you can either try the Second Quest (a sort of DLC released by Morphcat) or Micro Mages Maker!
OverObj
- physical (sold out, no other way to buy this game)
(All right, this game is no longer available from its original seller. But it’s too good for me not to mention it. Maybe there will be another way to purchase it some day.)
In Europe and America, the predominant genre for modern NES games is platformers. In the Japanese scene, shmups abound (or “STG” as they call them over there, “Shoo Ting Game”). Amidst Japanese shmups, a particularly popular genre is the bullet hell. Complex bullet patterns on screen and the primary gameplay is carefully weaving your spaceship or character through the bullets while you continuously fire back.
On the Famicom, bullet hell games are difficult to develop because the NES has room for only 64 sprite entries in its graphics memory, and can display only eight sprites per horizontal row. Nintendo developers used to refer to these sprites as “OBJ”.
Game developer Little Sound thus found a little trick to get over the OBJ limit: split the sprites across the middle of the screen and update the sprite data partway through rendering a frame.
It sounds like a tech demo, but this game is actually loads of fun. The difficulty quickly ramps up, but every time my little spaceship blows up, it feels like it was a genuine mistake and the game is being fair. The later bosses and levels do suffer from some sprite flickering despite OverObj’s technical feats. I love weaving through the bullet patterns, and that’s what keeps me coming back.
There’s also some excuse plot about how the Earth, after being abandoned by humans, is being attacked by mindless Objects that started breeding on their own, while being defended by lonely android who is waiting for makers who will never return.
Yeah, heavy stuff. Anyway, weave through the bullet storm, shoot back, and marvel at the joy of a well-made bullet hell on the NES.
Project Blue
Project Blue is mechanically simply a platformer. Run, jump, shoot, get to the end of the level. There is no scrolling, but there is a sprawling map to explore, which is mostly linear. There is not much in the way of branching paths. The mechanics are good enough with a peculiarity about jumping where you need to get a running start to gain more jumping speed. It works well enough.
Where the game really stands out is in its presentation. The box art and manual were made by the likes of M-Tee, a veteran of modern NES art. The level design and graphics were made by FrankenGraphics, a designer with a unique sense of dark, detailed artistry who has been working on the NEXXT tool for NES graphics. Her style is instantly recognisable as she is able to cram surprising amounts of detail that seem impossible for the limitations of the NES. Rounding up the team is toggleswitch who handled the code and design.
Together they created a cyberpunk story about Blue who is a test subject escaping the OmniCorp labs by jumping and shooting and dealing with the literal Think Tanks of OmniCorp’s board of directors.
The visuals are interesting and do give the sense of an oppressive biotech lab run by robots and cyborgs, as well as some outdoor shots of Neo Hong Kong where the story unfolds.
The music does a great job setting the foreboding atmosphere. I also get quite the kick out of its PDF manual, which is presented as being a scan of the actual manual. It looks like it was scanned in some dingy basement, because a cockroach managed to sneak onto the scanner bed!
The gameplay is somewhat comparable to Battle Kid but much more forgiving. Less trial-and-death gameplay, more fun running around dodging and shooting.
Sam’s Journey
Sam’s Journey tells the tale of little Sam who one day gets snatched from his bedroom and lands in a strange and wondrous land that he must make his way out of! Along the way he can find several outfits that all have their own special abilities.
