Where was Nvidia’s security team?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
00:08:07 UTC
09:08:07 KST

Outsiders had little reason to notice when Nvidia’s longtime chief security officer and head of product security stepped down in August. Just two and a half months later, CEO Jensen Huang himself left the company over growing questions about his extramarital relationships — starting with an executive at Megaspeed, a Singapore data center company that U.S. investigators suspect of funneling Nvidia chips to China, then widening to reports about two separate affairs with Nvidia employees.
At the time, David Reber, who spent four years as CSO and seven at Nvidia overall, said he was leaving for health reasons. “This wasn’t a pivot I had planned. But I know what needs to come first: family and health,” he wrote on LinkedIn in late August, disclosing that he’d been diagnosed with young-onset Parkinson’s more than two years ago.
Reber’s replacement, Saša Zdjelar, previously spent 17 years at ExxonMobil in Houston, leaving as a senior IT manager before spending one year at Salesforce and several years at the cybersecurity company ReversingLabs. Zdjelar didn’t respond to questions Thursday about the transition or what the security team knew.
Nothing public connects Reber’s or Zdjelar’s role changes to the Megaspeed matter, but it seems fair to wonder what Nvidia’s security team knew about the government’s scrutiny of Megaspeed and about Huang’s relationships, and whether anyone raised concerns. Reber didn’t respond to questions Thursday. Nvidia also has not answered our related questions.
Relationships between a CEO and his employees typically fall to HR, legal and the board, not security. But security specialists say any affair — even one inside the office — can give a foreign intelligence service leverage over an exec who wants it kept quiet. And Huang’s relationship with an executive at Megaspeed, a company under federal investigation, wasn’t an office matter at all. (Megaspeed has denied any wrongdoing.)
Still, spotting the clues isn’t easy. The way intelligence services collect information, much of it through everyday conversation rather than hacking, makes it especially difficult for security teams to identify risks, according to retired senior intelligence officer Nicholas Eftimiades, who says he has trained more than 1,000 corporate security professionals.
Some people inside Nvidia clearly knew parts of the picture. Nvidia’s top lawyer was engaged with the government’s Megaspeed inquiry more than a year ago, The New York Times reported earlier this week. (Nvidia has said it regularly contacts officials.) Reports about Huang’s relationships with employees suggest colleagues also knew of them, even if the behavior is not expressly forbidden by Nvidia’s code of conduct. The Times cited five Nvidia employees who said he was involved with a vice president who reported directly to him, and Reuters, citing two people, later reported a second relationship. What’s unclear is whether any of that reached security.
Even if it had reached Nvidia’s security team, Eftimiades, who wrote Chinese Espionage Operations and Tactics in 2020, said a security team’s resources are often limited. For one thing, they’re often underfunded because, unlike some other cost centers, they don’t drive revenue. It’s astonishingly common for CEOs to answer a security team’s warnings with “not a nickel more than compliance,” he said.
Many companies also define security overly narrowly, specialists say. In an opinion piece in The Hill, Don Aviv, CEO of the corporate investigations firm Interfor International, and author Jeremy Hurewitz, wrote, “Too many companies still approach security primarily as a matter of protecting facilities, preventing cyberattacks, and responding to physical threats.” The two argued that “[s]ecurity teams need to pay greater attention to insider threats, including the cultivation of executives and employees by foreign intelligence services, suspicious business relationships, conflicts of interest and attempts to obtain sensitive information through trusted personal contacts.”
It would be surprising if Nvidia’s security team didn’t have a more expansive remit than most companies given its scale and business partners, but it’s possible.
In fairness, some of it is simply beyond what any team can do. Speaking generally, not about Huang, Eftimiades offered a hypothetical in which a CEO complains that he can’t do something because “I’m fighting a board who doesn’t want to do this.” That tells an intelligence asset who the obstacle is. The service then asks, “Who’s the sticking point here?” and looks for ways into that person’s email and phone.
What a rival intelligence service wants is often less technology than policy insight, including who’s pushing to change export rules and who the biggest blocker is. “Intel world tends to be like cancer,” he said. “Once it gets into the body, it looks to spread and looks to reach out.”
The playbook isn’t new, Eftimiades added, “particularly for China.” Any executive dealing with the country “will tell you that they’ve been approached.” He said there are also “tons of diplomat stories” of U.S. diplomats walking into a hotel room to find a woman on the bed. He said what’s newer compared with Soviet era spying is the relationships these services cultivate now often last years, not weeks.
Technology can augment that human intelligence, Eftimiades said, pointing to IMSI catchers as one example. The devices mimic a cell tower so that nearby phones connect to them. A basic version can cost about $1,000 and fit in a suitcase, he said, though government-grade models cost far more, and there were a couple of cases involving Chinese espionage where these factored in last year.
Eftimiades says no one knows yet if the devices were used against Huang, whose phone was presumably hardened such that compromises could be detected, if not stopped altogether. But what Nvidia’s security team knew, and whether it could have acted on it, will only become clearer as the board’s independent investigation and federal inquiries proceed.
In the meantime, Eftimiades said he hopes the whole episode is “a wake-up call” for U.S. industry, which he said is mostly “clueless” about China’s tactics. “We have lost trillions [of dollars] in intellectual property,” is his guess. “Whatever we think we’re doing isn’t working.”

America Officially Loses Its Measles-elimination Status After Continued Spread of the Disease
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
23:37:00 UTC

America Officially Loses Its Measles-elimination Status After Continued Spread of the Disease (msn.com) 80
			
		 	
				Reuters reports:
The Pan American Health Organization has revoked the United States' measles-elimination status, ending a 26-year run after determining the country failed to halt continuous transmission of the highly contagious virus for more than a year, the Wall Street Journal reported on Friday...
The reported decision by the Americas branch of the World Health Organization comes as the US battles the highest number of measles cases it has seen in more than three decades. Some 7,500 people in the US have been infected with measles and 13 have died since 2025. That period coincides with falling immunization rates and rising vaccine hesitancy under US Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who has for years spread doubts, without scientific evidence, about the safety of measles vaccines...
Canada, Spain and the UK recently lost measles-elimination status after review by WHO experts....
"It is a fully preventable disease. It is unconscionable that we have this amount of measles," said Noel Brewer, a public health professor at the University of North Carolina...  William Moss, an epidemiology professor at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, said loss of measles elimination was a hit to the United States' reputation as a public health powerhouse... For the average American, he added, it means that the United States will continue to have large outbreaks, putting people at risk of disease, hospitalization and death.

Manjaro Linux Moves Toward Independent Community Governance
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
22:37:00 UTC
07:37:00 KST

Manjaro Linux Moves Toward Independent Community Governance (linuxiac.com) 2
			
		 	
				Arch-based Manjaro Linux "has announced that Manjaro Project e.V. is now officially registered as a non-profit in Germany," reports Linuxiac, "and is therefore a legally separate entity from the commercial Manjaro GmbH & Co. KG... after months of internal wrangling over who was in charge of the project, who had access to the infrastructure and who didn't, and the relationship between the dev team and the commercial company..."
The restructuring goes back to March, when the Manjaro developers released the Manjaro 2.0 Manifesto, which was a very public critique of the way Manjaro was being run.  The document claims that decision-making has effectively become the sole domain of the project founder, Philip Müller, with the other developers having very limited access to the infrastructure. There's also a concern about the drop in participation, the persistent technical issues, and the continued prioritization of commercial interests over the actual development of the distribution...
The manifesto proposed the transfer of all project infrastructure, domains, repositories and community funds to the new organization, together with arrangements for the use of the Manjaro trademark. The contributors were warning that they might well consider forking the distro if the talks didn't go their way. Müller then indicated that he'd be happy to talk about the proposed structure once the non-profit was in place. The association is now formally registered, and so one of the key aims of the manifesto has been realized... 
Perhaps the most important point is the actual evolution of the distribution. The most recent testing and stable updates were kicked off by the Manjaro team themselves, without any oversight from Müller. This points to the developers having already started to operate on their own in an area which was previously the preserve of the project's founder. They're also in the process of optimizing their dev infrastructure, in part as a response to the continued automated bot attacks on the existing infrastructure. 
Thanks to Slashdot reader king*jojo for sharing the news.

Watch SpaceX successfully execute the first ‘chopstick’ catch of Starship
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
21:44:44 UTC
06:44:44 KST

After aborting a planned catch of Starship Flight 15’s booster, SpaceX successfully captured Starship with its “Mechazilla” launch and catch tower for the first time. The company has performed ‘chopstick’ catches of its boosters before, but this is the first time the maneuver has successfully been executed on the Starship vehicle itself.
Watch SpaceX successfully execute the first ‘chopstick’ catch of Starship
SpaceX captured Starship using the arms of ‘Mechazilla’ its launch and catch tower for the first time.
SpaceX CEO Elon Musk said during the company’s third-quarter earnings call that “success is not guaranteed on these first flights, but I’d say it’s more likely than not that this succeeds next flight.” While not a complete success, in that the company was unable to recover the entire launch vehicle, it still stands as a milestone for the Starship program. And, since it has previously caught boosters on previous launches, there is reason to believe the failure today was not an insurmountable obstacle. SpaceX is still trying to determine what caused the booster to abort its landing and detonate over the Gulf of Mexico.
In the video above, you can watch Starship plunge through the clouds, flip vertically, and, with surprising grace, ease itself between the waiting arms of Mechazilla at around 4:20 PM ET.

Elon Musk, other tech execs sign petition calling on Nvidia to reinstate Jensen Huang
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
21:41:22 UTC
06:41:22 KST

A petition calling on Nvidia to reinstate the company’s recently ousted CEO Jensen Huang appears to have attracted more than 115,000 signatures as of Saturday afternoon.
The petition website said the signers include 5,965 current Nvidia employees, 1,108 former employees, and 63,063 shareholders. Notable supporters include Tesla and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk, Replit CEO Amjad Masad, Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney, and Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan.
The petition states that its signers are “concerned that Jensen was removed without meaningful due process” and demands that the board “bring him back while an independent investigation establishes the facts.” It also calls on the board to ensure a fair investigation and provide more information to the public.
“Jensen deserves due process, not mob justice,” the petition says.
Huang resigned on Thursday, four days after confessing to having an affair with a chip executive suspected by U.S. intelligence to be a Chinese spy. The former CEO has agreed to testify before Congress, while news reports have also suggested that Huang engaged in multiple extramarital affairs.
Nvidia appointed CFO Colette Kress as the company’s interim CEO while it searches for a permanent replacement. Nvidia’s stock price fell sharply through the week, while Huang’s departure is also expected to leave a big gap in AI industry funding.

====================================================================================================

EFF Criticizes US Government's Use of AI For Evaluating Senior Health Insurance Requests
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
21:00:00 UTC
06:00:00 KST

EFF Criticizes US Government's Use of AI For Evaluating Senior Health Insurance Requests (eff.org) 41
			
		 	
				"When we need medical care, we expect decisions about our treatment to be made by our doctors, not a tech company's algorithm," writes the EFF newsletter EFFector.
But in January America's federal government launched a program called WISeR that uses AI to evaluate authorization requests for certain procedures from America's federal health insurance program for seniors, Medicare.  EFF sued for records and discovered "additional issues" with the program that they say "could jeopardize Medicare beneficiaries' access to care."
 The records we received revealed that WISeR has resulted in widespread delays and denials of care, operational chaos, and reports of patient harm. Further documents obtained by EFF show a rushed and rocky rollout, highlighting the dangers that arise when governments treat AI like a silver bullet for complex problems.
EFF has now published hundreds of pages of documents obtained through our lawsuit, available here...    The documents also highlight the need for more transparency about safeguards against inappropriate, AI-driven denials. EFF has long argued that AI has no place in consequential government decisions about peoples' rights or access to benefits, such as immigration enforcement and policing decisions. That's because using automated decision-making in these contexts risks decisions that violate people's rights or deprive them of benefits they're entitled to based on biased or inappropriate considerations and without proper due process or accountability if there is an error.   By outsourcing decision-making to private AI companies without sufficient transparency, testing, or legal limits, WISeR jeopardizes seniors' access to medical care. Congress should step in to place greater legal limits on the use of automated decision-making in Medicare.  
The new documents "show why we need stronger legal limits around government use of automated decision-making..." according to the newsletter.  And another EFF page argues that the American public "still lacks the information needed to assess whether clinician review is providing meaningful oversight of AI systems and whether appropriate data is being used to evaluate prior authorization requests."
WISeR's payment structure gives vendors, as the government put it, "an incentive to deny as many claims as possible...."   [One company] denied more requests than it approved in the first few months of the program, and CMS inquired about "denials that appear inaccurate or inconsistent with applicable criteria."  Ultimately, the records obtained by EFF raise more questions than they answer about the safeguards used by WISeR vendors...
A growing body of scholarship points to the substantial limitations of "human in the loop" policies as guardrails for AI systems. AI-generated recommendations can unduly influence human decisions, leading reviewers to over-rely on automated judgements and rubber stamp them as correct. Even worse, at least one health insurance company actively pressured clinical reviewers to accept its AI systems' recommendations. Another company built a system that allowed clinical reviewers to reject claims without opening patient files; clinical reviewers using this system spent an average of just 1.2 seconds on each case.. 
As the government expands its use of automated decision-making, the potential for consequential mistakes increases rapidly. The records we've obtained so far raise questions about the rushed rollout and lack of guardrails for automated decision-making by the U.S. government. EFF urges transparency, oversight, and legal limits to protect people from the harms of automated decision-making in high risk systems.

Tesla opens Cybercab pre-orders ‘for personal use,’ with geo restrictions and a $299 monthly fee
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
20:04:44 UTC
05:04:44 KST

Tesla said on Friday evening that “select customers” now have the opportunity to pre-order the company’s autonomous, golden Cybercab “for personal use.”
More details became clear as those select customers looked at the order page. Tech reviewer Marques Brownlee posted that buyers “must accept delivery” in one of a handful of Texas cities and that the Cybercab will only operate in certain geofenced areas. The estimated delivery date is December 2026.
In addition to the $29,990 price tag, Cybercab owners will need to pay $299 per month to operate their vehicle.
In response to Brownlee’s post, Tesla CEO Elon Musk said the Cybercab “will be available nationwide, as we receive regulatory approval.” He added that the monthly fee is “too high”: “Should match the cost of Tesla [Full Self-Driving] and connectivity for other models, which is more like ~$115/month.”
Tesla’s Ashok Elluswamy offered more information about the geofencing, saying that Cybercab will work “unsupervised inside all the same geo locations as @robotaxi, just serving the owners’ needs.”
After falling far short of projected sales, Tesla recently announced it will cease Cybertruck production next year and use the additional factory capacity to build more Cybercabs.

Hundreds of Fake Accounts Defended Jensen Huang On Social Media
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
20:00:00 UTC
05:00:00 KST

Hundreds of Fake Accounts Defended Jensen Huang On Social Media (msn.com) 61
			
		 	
				The Washington Post reports on something peculiar about support for Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang online after he'd told the world he'd been unfaithful to his wife (before reports of his second extramarital affair):
Joining the chorus online were voices of seemingly everyday people: a chef, a stock trader, a Hebrew teacher and several environmental activists. But at least 400 of them were fake, according to research from the nonprofit Midas Project, with links to a public relations firm involved in previous so-called "sockpuppet" campaigns...  Many of the fake accounts, according to Midas Project executive director Tyler Johnston, replied to journalists, former U.S. officials and other prominent figures and backed not just Huang but Nvidia's push to sell its cutting-edge chips to Chinese firms. 
Like other documented influence campaigns, the posts appeared to have limited reach; the most-viewed tweet garnered about 8,000 views and 32 likes.  Still, some of the posts targeted lawmakers, including accounts for the [U.S.] House Select Committee on China, Republicans on the House Foreign Affairs Committee and Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-Massachusetts). "That's a place where you might not need a lot of views, but maybe the staffers who are running the lawmakers' account are paying attention to how their messages are received," said Johnston, whose group advocates for safe AI development...
There were other clues that helped identify accounts as being part of an apparently coordinated campaign, according to the Midas Project. Some of them were created minutes or hours apart, and had user names and profile photos refreshed at similar times. They also sometimes engaged on oddly specific issues ranging from Qatar to Nippon Steel's bid for U.S. Steel and a waste management plant in Oregon.... Any one one of the posts — often replies to other posts — might have not looked suspicious in isolation. But the various clues taken together, Johnston said, gave evidence that they were linked. Some of the accounts then rushed to delete posts after he posted his findings online, and at least 260 of them have been deactivated or suspended by X.

Magic: The Gathering is getting a Netflix animated series. For real this time.
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
19:47:40 UTC
04:47:40 KST

“It’s really happening. I promise. For real.” Those are the first words from Roy Graham, Head of Story at Magic: The Gathering, in a video posted yesterday announcing Planeswalker, the animated series heading to Netflix in 2027.
Magic: The Gathering is getting a Netflix animated series. For real this time.
Magic The Gathering: Planeswalker is set to debut on Netflix in 2027.
An animated Magic: The Gathering series was first teased back in 2019 with Joe and Anthony Russo at the helm. But that fell apart. In 2024, Netflix announced it still planned to make a series based on the game, but it decided to rework it from the ground up.
Now fans are finally getting their first glimpse of the show, which will be called Planeswalker. According to the sneak peek, because each plane exists in its own pocket universe, it will have a unique design, animation, and filmmaking style.
According to a synopsis of the show shared with IGN, Planeswalker will focus on Chandra Nalaar, a “volatile pyromancer hunted for her gifts, and haunted by her past.” Storyline details beyond that are vague, other than mentioning an “insidious force” that Chandra and other Planeswalkers will team up to confront “to prevent a cataclysmic war that threatens to consume their very existence.”

====================================================================================================

Dartmouth (Guarini) will not admit math PhD cohort this year
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
19:23:40 UTC
04:23:40 KST

The Guarini School of Graduate and Advanced Studies has suspended admissions to the next cohort in its math Ph.D. program, which would matriculate in the 2027-2028 academic year. Guarini dean Carrie Colla ’01 and College spokesperson Jana Barnello did not specify when or whether the program would resume admissions.
Colla wrote in a Nov. 10 email statement to The Dartmouth that Guarini “regularly considers how to best invest in graduate education.”
“As part of those regular reviews, we are pausing admissions to the math Ph.D. program for the 2027-2028 cycle while the School of Arts and Sciences conducts a department review,” she wrote.
The decision to suspend the program “was made in close partnership with leaders in the School of Arts and Sciences and we look forward to working collaboratively with the department on this review,” Colla added.
She wrote in a Nov. 10 email to current graduate students that there “will be opportunities for students to participate in the review” and that she would meet with math graduate students and department faculty on Nov. 17. Graduate students can also reach out to math department chair Dimitrios Giannakis and math professor Vladimir Chernov to share questions and concerns, she wrote.
“While I know it is disappointing news that your cohort will not be growing next year, I am confident that this approach will allow us to strengthen the experience of both our Ph.D. students and the undergraduate students in the department,” Colla wrote.
During a Q&A at the Nov. 9 general faculty meeting, which The Dartmouth attended but was not permitted to record, faculty raised concerns about the suspension. Math professor Peter Winkler, who opposed the suspension, said at the meeting the decision was communicated to math department faculty on Nov. 6. Winkler declined to provide additional comment after the meeting.
The Ph.D. program’s pause follows a national trend of decreasing Ph.D. program seats. Data collected from 55 undisclosed research institutions demonstrated that Ph.D. acceptances dropped 15% in 2026 from the previous year, even though domestic applications increased.
In a Nov. 10 email to current graduate students, which was obtained and reviewed by The Dartmouth, Giannakis wrote that faculty members were “continuing to engage” with College administrators to “find a path forward” that would “eventually resume” the Ph.D. program.
Giannakis added in the email that Colla and School of Arts and Sciences dean Nina Pavcnik would attend a math department faculty meeting on Nov. 11 to discuss the suspension. 
“While I do not expect a reversal of the decision to pause admissions, I hope there will at least be a constructive dialogue with actionable outcomes,” he wrote. 
Giannakis was unable to comment by time of publication.
The Guarini admissions office communicated the decision to applicants on Nov. 10, according to an email obtained by The Dartmouth. Any materials or recommendation letters that have already been submitted “will not be reviewed,” the Guarini admissions office wrote. Applications for the program, which typically accepts seven to nine students per year, would have been due on Dec. 15, 2026, according to the math department website.
“We recognize this is disappointing news and we are sorry for the disruption to your plans,” the email to people who already submitted application materials reads. 
On Nov. 10, Colla wrote to current math graduate students that “status and support” for current students “are not changing” and that the program will see “every currently enrolled student through graduation,” according to an email obtained and reviewed by The Dartmouth.
“Stipends, insurance and the five-year funding guarantee all remain in place for students making satisfactory academic progress,” Colla wrote. “Advising relationships, qualifying exams, degree requirements and teaching and research appointments will continue as planned.”
Guarini Ph.D. students received an annual stipend of $49,862.40 for the 2026-2027 academic year, along with tuition remission, health insurance and other benefits, according to the Guarini website. The College guarantees five years of financial support to students making satisfactory academic progress.
Graduate students typically teach “one course in each of their final three years of study,” according to the math department graduate student handbook. Starting this term, math Ph.D. candidates must also serve as a teaching assistant for one term each year after their third year, while first and second-year Ph.D. candidates must serve as teaching assistants for three terms a year instead of the previously required two, according to the handbook.
Third-year math Ph.D. candidate Jacob Lehmann Duke GR said he and his fellow graduate students learned of the suspension from Colla’s Nov. 10 email. He said he was “frustrated” by the “lack of transparency” surrounding the decision. 
“We were all shocked,” he said. “There was no indication that this was coming.”
Second-year math Ph.D. candidate Alex Moon GR said they believe suspending applications for the upcoming cycle sends “a message of instability” to future applicants.
“Part of the deal of graduate school is that you want there to be minimal factors outside of your control that will prevent you from getting this Ph.D.,” Moon said. “If this is something they do once, it’s something they can do later.”
In an interview with The Dartmouth, second-year math Ph.D. candidate Aidan Hennessey GR said “losing out on a cohort of students” would negatively affect the discussions among the “already quite small” Ph.D. program.
“Having people in adjacent areas of math to you — that you can talk about math and learn from, teach and talk through ideas with — all of this is very helpful,” Hennessey said. “Having more students around is nice for community.”
Lehmann Duke added that the suspension could put undergraduates intending to enroll in graduate-level mathematics courses “at a real deficit.”
Ben Singer ’27 — who said they have taken graduate-level math courses since their freshman year — added that there are “a few core graduate courses” that are “really important for undergraduates here who want to get into graduate school.” A decrease in Ph.D. students raises questions about whether those classes will be offered, whether undergraduates will be able to take them and how fewer course offerings will affect people who want to go to graduate school, Singer said.
The College “reserves the right to make changes in the offering” or cancel a course with fewer than five enrolled students, according to the Arts and Sciences Registrar’s Office website.
First-year computational science and modeling Ph.D. candidate Ethan Baird GR said “almost half” of his program’s coursework requirements are typically fulfilled through mathematics department courses. Baird added that these classes are the “basis of everything that we end up doing later,” which leads him to worry that the suspension of the admissions will have trickle-down effects on the computational science and modeling Ph.D. programs. 
Math major Ribhu Hooja ’27 said the department “did not officially” inform undergraduate students about the pause and that he instead found out from graduate students. If the pause lasts “even for a year, there could still be harm” because of “the relationships formed” between undergraduate and graduate students in advanced math classes, Hooja said.
“We learn how to apply to grad school, and what it means,” Hooja said. “It’s an important relationship I’ve really benefitted a lot from.”
Third-year Ph.D. candidate Michaela Polley GR said she was concerned about the pause’s effects on “research culture” among Dartmouth graduates and undergraduates alike.
“The idea of being a research university and a liberal arts college doesn’t work if you don’t have graduate students,” she said.
During the Nov. 9 meeting, College President Sian Leah Beilock and Pavcnik also addressed faculty concerns about the College’s discontinuation of the Society of Fellows, a three-year interdisciplinary research program established in 2014 to support postdoctoral research and scholarship. The Society has stopped recruiting new fellows and will conclude in summer 2028, when its current cohort completes their appointments, according to the program’s website. 
Beilock said at the meeting that the decision follows the need to reinvest funding in other institutional priorities.
Beilock announced at the meeting that the College is “working to reduce Dartmouth’s spending by $50 million,” in order to increase faculty and staff salaries “especially if and when inflation continues to rise,” according to a transcript of her speech published on the Office of the President’s website.
In a Nov. 11 email statement to The Dartmouth, Barnello wrote that the $50 million “reflects adjustments already made” to the budget, and that “roughly half” is “already in place” for fiscal year 2027.
Beilock also announced at the meeting that the College is “preparing” to convert North Park apartments, which are currently listed on the Office of Residential Life website as undergraduate housing, to graduate student housing. The North Park apartments have exclusively housed undergraduates since the 2022-2023 academic year, according to the Dartmouth Graduate Student Council website.
Correction appended (Nov. 12, 9:30 a.m.): A previous version of this article referred to Alex Moon as he. They use they/them pronouns.
Sahil Gandhi ’29 is a reporter from Staten Island, N.Y., and is majoring in environmental studies and government modified with philosophy and economics. He loves word searches and falling down internet and Wikipedia rabbit holes. He can be reached at sgandhi@thedartmouth.com.

Amazon Expands Drone Deliveries, and Gets Noise Complaints. 'Like a Swarm of Bees'
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
19:00:00 UTC
04:00:00 KST

Amazon Expands Drone Deliveries, and Gets Noise Complaints. 'Like a Swarm of Bees' (chron.com) 26
			
		 	
				"In August, Amazon announced it was expanding its airborne shipping to suburban areas in nearly 500 U.S. cities," reports the Chicago Sun-Times, "including the Chicago, Atlanta, Cleveland and Boise, Idaho, metro areas."  And on Tuesday Amazon cut the ribbon on its new drone delivery center Tuesday 24 miles south of Chicago.
But Amazon's drones are already servicing parts of Texas, notes the Houston Chronicle.  And  north of Houston, Theresa Gibbons complains "It was kind of cool and fascinating at first, but now it is driving me crazy."
Her home in the Bridgestone neighborhood is nestled right in the flight path of Amazon's drone service. Gibson said she hears roughly a dozen drones flying over her home each day.   Noise pollution has become a frequent complaint in Texas neighborhoods where the service operates. Residents have compared the sound to everything from a massive beehive to a full-on air raid. "It does not sound like a [single] bumble bee. It sounds like a large swarm of bees at a much higher volume than a normal swarm of bees. And it's all day long," said Gibson.
Amazon's drone delivery service is currently operating in four Texas cities, including Houston, Waco, San Antonio and Richardson. The company announced major expansion plans in August, but growing complaints in pilot neighborhoods could foreshadow complications later down the line, with regions such as Harris County already taking legal action in response... So far, a group of fifteen states, plus Harris County,  [Texas] have formed a coalition that has filed a petition in the US Court of Appeals challenging the Federal Aviation Administration's environmental review, citing that it failed to adequately address the effects of expanding drone deliveries.    
And meanwhile near Boise, Idaho, local news station KTVB reports....
Less than two weeks after Amazon began delivering packages by drone in Nampa, the cities of Nampa and Meridian say they have received complaints over the noise and how often the drones fly...  Amazon began Prime Air drone deliveries Oct. 30 from its East Franklin Road fulfillment center, making Nampa the company's first Idaho drone site... 
How people feel about the drones depends on whom you ask. A Meridian man who has received drone deliveries says the noise doesn't bother him. Neighbors closest to the launch site say the drones fly from sunup to sundown and are disturbing their peace.  Randy Nye, who has lived in Cherry Creek Estates for 17 years, compared it to living in a beehive.  "It's just a constant presence," he said....  Nye, who works from home, said he and his neighbors who technically are just outside city limits had no idea this was coming. He said the city approved the drones in May, and they were never given written notice this was coming, despite living so close. He said they were shocked when deliveries started by drones just 12 days ago.  Nye said he hopes something can change. 
A few homes down, Kerri Wattier has started counting. On Wednesday with KTVB, she clicked a handheld counter each time a drone passed over her home. She started at 3:30 p.m. By 4:18 p.m., she had counted 39.  "It sounds like lawnmowers and leaf blowers flying over our house," Wattier said.  
"Amazon says it is evaluating changes, including adjusting flight paths," the article adds.
Ironically at that ribbon-cutting ceremony near Chicago, the speakers were occasionally drowned out by the roar of the drones, reports the Chicago Tribune, since they're "relatively loud when they are close to the ground."  ("At the point of delivery they top out at 68 decibels," said Amazon economic development manager Sam Bailey, adding that "It's very brief exposure and it only occurs during daytime hours.")
But that site has been operational since October 12, and has "generally" hit its maximum capacity of 300 drone orders daily, Bailey told the Chicago Tribune, adding that scaling up is "something that we're always considering."  And since October 12 they've already seen "thousands" of orders delivered by their 21 drones just across that 7.5-mile radius:
Bailey said customers should clear the area when a drone delivery is incoming. The drones drop packages from about 12 feet. If the drone detects movement in the area, he said, the delivery will be canceled.  "You want your kids inside the house," Bailey said. "We will not drop a package in an unsafe location. People who want to stand below and try to catch it, don't do that." 
The Chicago Sun-Times points out that Walmart already offers some drone deliveries from over 75 stores across Arkansas, Florida, North Carolina, Georgia and Texas, and "earlier this year said it planned to expand drone delivery service to 150 more stores in partnership with Wing, a division of Google parent company Alphabet, giving it 270 drone delivery locations by 2027."

====================================================================================================

Got an iPhone Duo? These 9 settings make it easier to use
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
18:00:00 UTC
03:00:00 KST

Apple’s new foldable, the iPhone Duo, has proven popular enough that the company can’t keep the device in stock. While the Duo introduces a new way to use an iPhone, getting the most out of this initial version may require adjusting a few settings to make it work better when it comes to everyday tasks, like unlocking the device with Touch ID, navigating the interface, and taking photos.
After spending some time with the Duo, we’d recommend making the following adjustments to make the new iPhone a bit easier to use, most notably when it comes to the use of Touch ID.
Settings to make it easier to live with Touch ID
Unlike modern iPhones, the Duo doesn’t support Face ID. That means you have to adjust to pressing a finger on the side of the device once again to authenticate with apps, unlock the device, and use Apple Pay. But there are a few things you can do to make the switch back to the older technology a bit less painful.
- Register extra fingerprints: This may seem like an obvious suggestion, but if you’re used to just registering a fingerprint or two with Touch ID, prepare to rethink that strategy with the Duo. Because the device can be held in multiple orientations, flipped both horizontally and vertically, and because of the small size of the Duo’s screen, you may find that a lot of your fingers (and thumbs!) can now reach the Touch ID button. We’d recommend holding the device in your hand and turning it in different ways, then register the fingerprints where you end up holding the device the most — on both hands. Yes, setting up Touch ID can be a bit tedious compared with Face ID, as there are multiple prints to register. Still, it’s a one-time setup, and later on you’ll be glad that you took the time to make more of your fingerprints work. To register a fingerprint, go to Settings > Touch ID & Passcode > Add a Fingerprint.
- Unlock with Apple Watch: If you’re in the market for the iPhone Duo, there’s a good likelihood that you own other Apple devices, like the Apple Watch. If that’s the case, you’re in luck because you can use your Watch to unlock the Duo without having to bother with Touch ID. This will save you time and frustration in the long run, so get used to wearing your Apple Watch more often, especially when you’re out and about and don’t want to fiddle as much with finger placement. To use the Apple Watch unlock, your watch will need to be passcode-protected and worn near your phone. To turn this feature on, go to Settings > Touch ID & Passcode > Unlock with Apple Watch.
- Rest finger to Open: This is an optional setting, not necessarily a “must” like the above, but we’ve found it useful. The “rest finger” setting is actually an accessibility feature, but it can double as a usability feature, too. When enabled, you only have to rest your finger lightly on the Touch ID sensor to unlock the device, instead of having to press down. Those who prefer this setting find it can make unlocking the phone faster and more convenient, but others may disagree, as they like the more tactile sensation of a button press. Try it both ways and see which one fits you best. To enable this one, go to Settings > Touch ID & Passcode > Rest Finger to Open.
Usability Improvements
There are a few settings that could also improve your experience with the iPhone Duo, as they may make it easier to perform certain everyday tasks, like taking screenshots or reaching different parts of the screen, among other things.
- Turn off Fixed Volume Controls: This is a favorite hack for new Duo owners, as it makes using the device in any orientation much easier. By default, the iPhone Duo’s volume buttons are “fixed,” which means that no matter how you rotate the device, the Volume Up button remains the button that increases the volume and the Volume Down button decreases it. But many people find it’s easier if the buttons’ functionality rotates with the device. That is, the buttons re-orient themselves based on how you are holding the phone, making the “up” option always on the top or right. As you fold, unfold, and flip the phone, having buttons that flip with you actually makes more sense. To turn off the fixed volume controls, go to Settings > Sounds & Haptics > Fixed Position Volume Controls.
- Back Tap for Screenshots: The Duo tries to make it easier to take a screenshot by pressing the side button and either the Volume Up or Volume Down button at the same time. However, you still might find the process a two-handed affair (unless you just ask Siri to do it for you). That’s why many Duo owners are turning to a buried Accessibility feature to make taking screenshots a bit less complicated. In the phone’s accessibility settings, you can configure whether a double tap or triple tap on the back of the device triggers a particular action — which you can set to ‘take a screenshot.’ (Some people prefer the triple tap so they don’t accidentally trigger the feature.) Once enabled, you could hold your Duo in one hand, then use a finger to tap on the back of the phone to take the screenshot, saving you a bit of the hassle that comes with managing the button combinations. To configure this, go to Settings > Accessibility > Touch > Back Tap > then choose a double or triple tap and your action. (Screenshot is under the “Device” menu.)
- Reachability: This is another accessibility feature that some Duo owners like to configure, but others like to have off. The option was originally designed to make larger-screened devices easier to use with one hand, which makes it sometimes hard to reach, depending on your hand size. Typically, when Reachability is turned on, the iPhone will lower the top half of the screen to make it easier to reach with your thumb. The Duo does things a little differently, though. Because the phone is wide — it’s shaped like a passport — it’s the far side of the screen that’s harder to reach than the top. So when you turn on Reachability, you will instead be able to swipe the left or right edge of the screen to bring a side into reach. We should note that this feature is enabled by default, but if you find yourself accidentally triggering it and want to turn it off, you can under Settings > Accessibility > Touch > Reachability.
- Lock when folded: This one is more of a personal preference, we’ll admit. But if you tend to unfold the device when you’re working with it, then snap it closed when you’re done, you might like to configure a setting that will automatically lock the screen for you when the device is folded shut. However, doing so requires you to create a new shortcut, as there isn’t a setting for this — at least, not yet. 
  - To make the shortcut, you’ll open the Shortcuts app, tap the plus (+) sign, then the Edit button in the upper-right. You then tap into the search bar, and tap on the word “automation” that appears in blue under the search bar that appears at the top of the screen. Next, you’ll scroll down until you find the option “Opened or Closed” under the Display section. Tap that, tap on the word “Opened” to change it to “Closed,” then tap in the search bar at the bottom again and type in “Lock Screen.” Tap on the option 
“Lock Screen” when it appears in the search results, and you’re done — the shortcut has been made. You can tap on the play button here to test it, if you’d like. And if you ever change your mind, just delete the shortcut!
Special Camera Tricks
The camera tricks are a little more niche, but some Duo owners have found the following to be helpful.
- Customize Camera Controls: The iPhone Duo lets you customize the on-screen layout of the camera controls in the Camera app itself, as part of the latest iOS software update. To use this feature, you’ll open the Camera app and open the six-dot Camera Controls menu in the bottom right. You then tap on the plus sign at the top of the menu (or to the right or left depending on how you’re holding the phone!). This lets you pick and choose which of the camera tools appear above the camera frame itself for quick access, like the flash, or the option to enable or disable Live Photos, or whether Night Mode is on or off, among other things. You can then drag those options to create whichever order makes the most sense for you. (Note: this feature is not exclusive to the Duo.)
- Disable Smart Take Flash: The Smart Take feature on the iPhone Duo is designed to help you take hands-free group or solo shots without having to use a self-timer. Instead, you prop up the Duo on a stable surface and activate Smart Take by pressing the button. The camera will detect when there’s movement or the poses change, and the subjects are ready, then takes the picture. But one thing not everyone likes is that each time the photo is captured, the camera flash blinks. This is meant to be a visual clue that lets you know the shot has been taken. However, you can control how the flash behaves during these automatic capture sessions by configuring a setting. To do so, you’ll go to Settings > Camera > Flash During Capture and toggle the switch off if you prefer.

America Cancels 4,000 More Flights.  FAA Denies Impact of New AI System.
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
18:00:00 UTC
03:00:00 KST

America Cancels 4,000 More Flights. FAA Denies Impact of New AI System. (kxan.com) 65
			
		 	
				UPDATE:  FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford said Saturday that U,S. airlines will likely cancel 4,000 to 5,000 of the roughly 41,000 flights scheduled for Saturday, reports Yahoo News. And as to the cause of the incident...
[U.S. Transportation Secretary Sean] Duffy said a router involved in the switch from copper wire to fiber "started flooding other routers in our air traffic control system." The system saw "over 1,000 connection attempts every four to five seconds," he said. That disrupted communications with airplanes and radar. 
America's nationwide grounding of flights on Friday happened "amid growing scrutiny... of a new AI-powered air traffic management system" deployed by America's Federal Aviation Administration, reports Texas news station KXAN.  
Ironically, that system is called SMART — but the outage happened "following warnings" from members of the U.S. Congress about this new AI system's safety, reliability and oversight:
In an Oct. 13 letter to the Government Accountability Office, more than 20 members of Congress requested an independent review of the FAA's development and implementation of its Strategic Management of Airspace, Routes and Trajectories system, known as SMART. The lawmakers questioned whether the technology was being deployed with adequate input from frontline air traffic controllers, sufficient training and appropriate safety evaluations...  In their letter to the GAO, the lawmakers specifically questioned whether the FAA had adequately evaluated risks associated with SMART's artificial intelligence capabilities, including the potential for the technology to interfere with experienced controllers' decision-making or generate inaccurate safety-critical information.  "The nation's frontline aviation safety workforce apparently had little to no input into SMART's development and have received limited training and information during the current deployment process," the lawmakers wrote. The letter also questioned the FAA's decision to initially pilot the technology in heavily congested airspace, including the National Capital Region.  
"Our switches, our routers, actually got overloaded with data," U.S. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy acknowledged Friday.
But FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford said Saturday that officials have found "zero evidence of anything AI related" in Friday's outage, according to Yahoo News.
An article from The Hill.com notes that Duffy also said the new AI tool had nothing to do with the ground stop, and that he said the system "isn't plugged in."
"This is completely disconnected, and I love that some people have said this would be the root cause. By the way, if it was, I'll tell you that, but this is not even plugged in," he said. "So, there's no possible way SMART would have impacted."
Duffy also asked Congress for $50 billion to rebuild what he described as an "incredibly old" air traffic control system.
 
Duffy had posted the day before that the AI system had been at least partially rolled out and that it was already working (adding "Soon this technology is going NATIONWIDE")
And Friday CNN's aviation analyst said they believed that AI system could be connected to the nationwide outage, calling it "a tremendous amount of coincidence," according to a report from Mediaite:
 CNN Aviation Analyst: "Many times in the past, what we have seen, and this has gone back decades, is that because air traffic control is this piecemeal, mishmash of a whole bunch of different systems...  some going back to radio, telephones and floppy disks... when they put in new computer systems, they often have difficulty talking with, communicating with, or working with the much older systems. And I have to say, it's a tremendous amount of coincidence...  If they're putting in new software now, this new SMART system to start managing that, and now we have a communications outage, I'm — we've seen this so many times in the past. I don't know if they're linked, but it's just too much coincidence that they were trying to start that moving today, and now we have problems with the en route system.
So if I was to guess — and we haven't heard any, you know, it's not that it's threats or criminals or terrorists. We haven't heard anything like that. So I am guessing that once again we have a problem with new air traffic control computer systems, AI — you know, modern systems trying to communicate with old systems.  And we have just seen this so many times in the past, and it's a big problem... Our whole national airspace system is you know, pieces and parts. It's kind of a Franken-system.  And that would be my guess, that they are having computer issues by putting the new software in with the old, just because today they announced that that were going to do this new system. 
KXAN describes some of the complexity of the newly-launched AI system:
According to the FAA, the system consolidates approximately 200 data streams, including airline schedules, flight paths, weather patterns, airport capacity, airspace conditions and air traffic controller staffing metrics. The agency says SMART uses artificial intelligence to analyze that information, predict traffic flows, identify potential conflicts and recommend adjustments to flight routes and departure schedules before disruptions occur. The FAA describes the technology as a shift from a reactive approach to a predictive one, allowing aviation officials to identify congestion, weather-related restrictions and other operational problems hours, days or even weeks in advance...
The congressional letter raised questions about whether the FAA's implementation strategy was moving faster than its safety evaluations, workforce training and independent oversight could support.
 
Ironically, the article points out that the FAA promoted anticipated benefits from the technology including fewer delays and flight cancellations.  (Other anticipated benefits included improved on-time performance and faster recovery from weather disruptions.)  Although the FAA has realized one of the system's anticipated benefits:  reduced fuel consumption.

The VHDL standard's float package gets sqrt(2) wrong
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
17:58:58 UTC
02:58:58 KST

We found sqrt(2.0) in the standard's float_pkg returns one unit in the last place too low in double precision by default. This is the IEEE 1076-2008 and 1076-2019 VHDL floating-point package; IEEE publishes the file, GHDL and NVC ship it unchanged.
The root uses a fixed count of Newton steps and no sticky bit. But taking it one bit at a time from the definition is exact, with a subtraction per bit, the same thing as square root by hand on paper.
Our check treats operands as exact fractions, does each operation exactly and rounds once before comparing. mac(-6, -1, -5), or (-6)(-1)+(-5), returns -15 instead of 1. Converting 2^-512 double to single returns 1.0; reciprocal of 2^127 in single returns infinity. For all operands and pairs in a 7-bit format (3 exponent bits, 3 fraction bits), plus all mac triples, we ran 8,692,326 calls, and 1,271,619 weren't correctly rounded.
The exact reference, testbench, causes with source line numbers and patch are in the folder. All 32,470,838 calls from four test sets pass with patch; GHDL and NVC produce identical result files on common sets. fixed_pkg division gets 41,026 of 751,418 wrong and has its patch there. Reported to IEEE P1076 working group (VHDL-Issues 388), ghdl 3486, nvc 1758, fphdl 4.
To see it yourself, with GHDL and nothing else:
ghdl -a --std=08 plain.vhd
ghdl --elab-run --std=08 plain
ieee.float_pkg is the floating-point package of the VHDL standard, IEEE Std 1076-2008 and 1076-2019. The source is the IEEE's own: ieee/float_generic_pkg-body.vhdl on the release branch of opensource.ieee.org/vasg/Packages, 5,712 lines, $Revision: 1220 $. The working group's develop branch at gitlab.com/IEEE-P1076/Packages has the same bytes, and GHDL and NVC ship them unchanged. The runs here are in GHDL 7.0.0-dev and NVC 1.23.1, and where both ran the same vectors they wrote the same result file.
The package is set beside exact arithmetic. An operand is a sign and a fraction of two integers, the operation is done on the fractions, and the result is rounded once into the format in the rounding style the call names. exact.py is that reference.
With the package's own settings: round_nearest, 3 guard bits, denormal numbers on.
call                                        returns                   exactly rounded
sqrt (2.0) in float64                       3FF6A09E667F3BCC          3FF6A09E667F3BCD
sqrt (14.0)                                 406F7750                  406F7751
sqrt (2.0**-149)                            4.2e-22                   3.7e-23
mac (-6.0, -1.0, -5.0)                      -15.0                     1.0
mac (-5.0, 1.0, 4.0)                        15.0                      -1.0
mac (1.0, 1.0, -infinity)                   +infinity                 -infinity
mac (1 + 2.0**-23, 1 - 2.0**-23, -1.0)      -2.0**-26                 -2.0**-46
resize (2.0**-512 in float64, 8, 23)        1.0                       0.0
resize (2.0**600 in float64, 8, 23)         2.0**88                   +infinity
resize (2.0**-1074 in float64, 8, 23)       +infinity                 0.0
resize (2.0**-650 in float64, 8, 23)        stops the simulator       0.0
reciprocal (2.0**127)                       +infinity                 2.0**-127
divide (2.0**-149, 2.0)                     2.0**-149                 0.0
dividebyp2 (3 * 2.0**-149, 2.0)             2.0**-149                 2.0**-148
scalb (1.0, 300)                            0.0                       +infinity
scalb (+infinity, -10)                      2.0**118                  +infinity
to_signed (0.50000006, 32)                  0                         1
to_unsigned (199.78125, 8)                  199                       200
And where the call names a rounding style:
divide (6.0, 3.0, round_inf)                2.0000002                 2.0
reciprocal (2.0, round_inf)                 0.50000006                0.5
add (largest, largest, round_zero)          +infinity                 largest
multiply (2.0**-100, 2.0**-100, round_inf)  0.0                       2.0**-149
add (1.0, -1.0, round_neginf)               +0.0                      -0.0
to_signed (2.25, 8, round_inf)              2                         3
to_float (100000, 5, 10, round_zero)        +infinity                 65504.0, the largest
plain.vhd makes these calls and prints WRONG beside each result that is not the exactly rounded one: 24 lines, and the 25th call stops the simulator with an index out of bounds at line 358 of the package body. It needs GHDL and nothing else:
ghdl -a --std=08 plain.vhd
ghdl --elab-run --std=08 plain
The plain operands are not rare ones. Of the integers 1 to 4,096, sqrt returns a float32 one unit off for 256 and a float64 one unit off for 264. Of the 4,913 triples made from -6 to 6, 0.25, 0.5, 1.5 and 10, mac returns the wrong float32 for 290. resize from float64 to float32 returns +infinity for every power of two from 2^-1074 to 2^-1024, stops the simulator from 2^-662 to 2^-639, returns 2^(e+512) from 2^-638 to 2^-512 and 2^(e-512) from 2^513 to 2^639.
10,000 calls per operation, format and rounding style, on operands aimed at the places arithmetic breaks: denormal operands and results, both ends of the exponent range, fractions that round up into the next binade, exact ties, signed zeros, infinities. A cell is the number of results that are not the exactly rounded one, of the calls compared. A conversion whose result does not fit the integer is not compared. The functions run are the ones in the tables; remainder, modulo, Logb, Nextafter, the comparisons, the conversions with real and with fixed point, the text input and output, operands of two different formats in one call, and check_error false were not run.
float32                            round_nearest      round_zero         round_neginf       round_inf
add                                0 of 10,000        304 of 10,000      312 of 10,000      159 of 10,000
subtract                           0 of 10,000        314 of 10,000      312 of 10,000      143 of 10,000
multiply                           0 of 10,000        1,102 of 10,000    781 of 10,000      775 of 10,000
divide                             27 of 10,000       670 of 10,000      2,324 of 10,000    2,395 of 10,000
sqrt                               735 of 10,000      581 of 10,000      542 of 10,000      774 of 10,000
mac                                2,671 of 10,000    3,697 of 10,000    3,213 of 10,000    3,302 of 10,000
reciprocal                         384 of 10,000      586 of 10,000      1,093 of 10,000    2,613 of 10,000
resize to a narrower format        422 of 20,000      2,728 of 20,000    2,516 of 20,000    2,466 of 20,000   272 more stop the simulator
resize to a wider format           0 of 20,000        0 of 20,000        0 of 20,000        0 of 20,000
to_float (signed, 32 and 64 bits)  0 of 20,000        0 of 20,000        0 of 20,000        0 of 20,000
to_signed (8, 32, 64 bits)         613 of 28,637      0 of 28,626        5,934 of 28,608    5,898 of 28,623
to_unsigned (8, 32, 64 bits)       280 of 16,506      0 of 17,133        0 of 14,493        5,964 of 17,145

to_float (integer)                 0 of 10,000        0 of 10,000        0 of 10,000        0 of 10,000

scalb                              2,653 of 10,000    3,088 of 10,000    3,381 of 10,000    3,401 of 10,000
float64                            round_nearest      round_zero         round_neginf       round_inf
add                                0 of 10,000        260 of 10,000      311 of 10,000      138 of 10,000
subtract                           0 of 10,000        283 of 10,000      317 of 10,000      147 of 10,000
multiply                           0 of 10,000        1,163 of 10,000    852 of 10,000      866 of 10,000
divide                             18 of 10,000       698 of 10,000      2,495 of 10,000    2,406 of 10,000
sqrt                               1,278 of 10,000    619 of 10,000      659 of 10,000      799 of 10,000
mac                                2,673 of 10,000    3,697 of 10,000    3,285 of 10,000    3,290 of 10,000
reciprocal                         350 of 10,000      477 of 10,000      1,087 of 10,000    2,623 of 10,000
resize to a narrower format        801 of 30,000      4,116 of 30,000    3,980 of 30,000    4,014 of 30,000   445 more stop the simulator
resize to a wider format           0 of 10,000        0 of 10,000        0 of 10,000        0 of 10,000
to_float (signed, 32 and 64 bits)  0 of 20,000        0 of 20,000        0 of 20,000        0 of 20,000
to_signed (8, 32, 64 bits)         618 of 28,219      0 of 28,231        6,161 of 28,340    5,952 of 28,262
to_unsigned (8, 32, 64 bits)       321 of 16,330      0 of 16,839        0 of 14,237        6,155 of 16,776
to_integer                         0 of 10,000        0 of 10,000        2,125 of 10,000    2,183 of 10,000
to_float (integer)                 0 of 10,000        0 of 10,000        0 of 10,000        0 of 10,000
dividebyp2                         1,543 of 10,000    1,902 of 10,000    2,321 of 10,000    2,391 of 10,000
scalb                              2,218 of 10,000    2,540 of 10,000    2,982 of 10,000    2,967 of 10,000
In float32 that is 81,068 wrong and 272 stops in 859,771 calls, and in float64 86,081 wrong and 445 stops in 857,234. The same calls in float16 give 113,980 wrong in 866,782, and in float128 90,744 wrong and 585 stops in 855,777.
Where a format is small enough, every operand there is:
format                                      calls                                             compared       wrong
7 bits, 3 of exponent and 3 of fraction     every operand, every pair, every triple of mac    8,692,326      1,271,619
10 bits, 4 of exponent and 5 of fraction    every operand and every pair                      17,340,682     883,716
float16                                     every operand of the one-operand functions        2,998,266      266,342
mac in round_nearest is wrong for 243,520 of the 2,097,152 triples of the 7-bit format.
The distance from the returned value to the exact result, in units of the last place of the exact result, for the round_nearest column of the float32 and float64 tables above. A correctly rounded result is at most 0.5 away.
float32                        wrong     within 1    1 to 1,000    over 1,000    wrong sign    infinity or NaN    sign of zero
divide                         27        27          0             0             0             0                  0
sqrt                           735       629         6             100           0             0                  0
mac                            2,671     163         34            347           2,018         41                 68
reciprocal                     384       0           0             0             0             360                24

to_unsigned                    280       280         0             0             0             0                  0

divide                         18        18          0             0             0             0                  0

mac                            2,673     137         17            372           2,045         11                 91
reciprocal                     350       0           0             0             0             327                23
resize to a narrower format    801       0           145           160           0             436                60
to_signed                      618       618         0             0             0             0                  0
to_unsigned                    321       321         0             0             0             0                  0
dividebyp2                     1,543     803         0             607           0             133                0
scalb                          2,218     879         1             637           0             701                0
all                            9,820     3,953       166           1,874         2,045         1,608              174
infinity or NaN counts an infinity or a NaN returned for a number, a number returned for an infinity or a NaN, and the wrong one of them. sign of zero is +0 for -0 or -0 for +0.
Of the 19,147 wrong results, 7,213 are within one unit. 4,063 have the wrong sign, 3,775 are more than 1,000 units away and 3,505 confuse a number with infinity or NaN. The 45 wrong quotients of divide are exact ties taken to the odd neighbour. sqrt (2.0) in float64 is 0.565 of a unit from the root, and the correctly rounded value is 0.435 from it; sqrt (14.0) is 0.544 from the root.
python3 exact.py sizes vectors.txt results.txt
Line numbers are those of the IEEE file.
normalize, line 422. Every result goes through exp := resize (expon, exp'length) + shiftr. The exponent comes in at the width of the caller's format and is cut to two bits more than the target's. From float64 to float32 that is ten bits, so the exponent is taken modulo 1,024: 2^-512 comes out as 1.0 and 2^600 as 2^88. Where the cut exponent lands among the denormal numbers, the shift count that follows indexes outside the fraction and the simulator stops. In the same function a result too large for the format is infinity in every rounding style and a result too small is zero in every rounding style. Rounding toward zero gives the largest number, and rounding away from zero gives the smallest.
divide, line 1243, and reciprocal, line 1099. The sticky bit handed to the rounding is the constant '1'. An exact quotient is rounded as if something were left over: one unit up in round_inf, and to the wrong side of an exact tie in round_nearest, which happens among denormal quotients.
reciprocal, lines 1053 to 1093. The exponent of the result, -e - 3, is kept in as many bits as e, and for the top binade it does not fit. In float32 every argument from 2^127 up returns infinity, and in float64 every argument from 2^1023 up. 1/-0 returns +infinity and 1/-infinity returns +0.
sqrt, line 1787. Newton's iteration from (1 + x)/2, fraction_width/4 + 1 steps in truncated fixed point, and the result goes to the rounding without a sticky bit. For a normal argument it ends on the root or one unit beside it: in the vectors above it is one unit off for 607 of 9,156 normal float32 arguments and 1,146 of 9,236 normal float64 ones, and never further. For a denormal argument the start is too far away for that many steps: 128 of 359 float32 denormals are wrong, 104 of them by more than two units, and sqrt (2.0**-149) returns 11 times the root.
mac, lines 1438 to 1536. The product is cut to the width of one operand before c is added (lines 1529 and 1536), so a sum that cancels returns what the cut left. Where the product and c have the same exponent and unlike signs and the product is the larger, line 1514 subtracts the larger from the smaller: (-6)(-1) + (-5) returns -15. An infinite c takes the sign of the product, and infinity minus infinity returns infinity (line 1438).
add, line 839. An exact zero from operands of unlike signs is +0 in every style. The paragraph the comment cites, IEEE 854 6.3, makes it -0 when rounding toward minus infinity.
to_signed, to_unsigned, lines 301 to 345, and to_integer, line 3193. Three bits below the integer are kept and the rest are dropped without a sticky bit, so 0.50000006 is taken for one half and goes to the even neighbour, 0. For a number that fills every bit of a to_unsigned result the three bits are zeros: 199.78125 into 8 bits returns 199. round_inf and round_neginf look at the half bit only, so 2.25 rounded up is 2.
resize, line 2407. -0 to a narrower format returns +0, and the comment says hate -0. To a wider format it stays -0.
to_float of an integer, lines 2676 and 2803. A number too large for the format returns infinity in every rounding style.
dividebyp2, line 1378, and Scalb, lines 4601 to 4610. Both hand the result to normalize with no guard bits, so a denormal result is cut off where it should be rounded. Scalb adds N in exponent_width + 1 bits, where a large N wraps, and it scales infinity like a number. dividebyp2 takes a denormal power of two for a divisor that is not a power of two.
With denormalize false. A zero is broken up with a higher exponent than a number of the lowest binade, so add of a zero and such a number of the opposite sign subtracts the number from zero and returns the wrapped difference. reciprocal treats those numbers as the denormals they are not.
float_generic_pkg-body.patch applies to the IEEE file. It changes the package body only, 33 hunks, and no declaration.
- normalize decides between zero, denormal, normal and infinity on the exponent at its full width, and returns the largest or the smallest number where the rounding style asks for it.
- divide and reciprocal take the sticky bit from the remainder of the division. reciprocal keeps its exponent two bits wider and gives zero and infinity the sign of the argument.
- sqrt takes the integer square root of the significand one bit per step, with the remainder as the sticky bit.
- mac places the whole product and c in one window of 3 * fraction_width + 9 bits, adds or subtracts there and rounds once. It is a fused multiply-add.
- The conversions to integers keep three bits of the number whatever the shift, collect the rest in a sticky bit, and round up in the directed styles on any bit below the integer.
- add returns the other operand when one is zero and gives an exact zero the sign the rounding style calls for. resize keeps the sign of zero.
- to_float of an integer returns the largest number where the style rounds toward zero.
- dividebyp2 and Scalb round a denormal result with guard bits. Scalb returns infinity and zero as they are and takes any N.
On the same vectors:
                                                          the package as shipped       patched
four formats, 10,000 per cell, 3,439,564 calls            371,873 wrong, 1,302 stop 0 wrong, 0 stop
the 7-bit format, 8,692,326 calls                         1,271,619 wrong              0
the 10-bit format, 17,340,682 calls                       883,716 wrong                0
float16, the one-operand functions, 2,998,266 calls       266,342 wrong                0
plain.vhd, 25 calls                                       24 wrong, 1 stop             0
GHDL and NVC wrote the same result files with either package: 8,693,248 lines for the 7-bit format and 3,680,000 for the four formats, the 1,302 stops on the same lines. The reference itself was set beside this machine's own arithmetic on the float32 and float64 vectors of add, subtract, multiply, divide and sqrt in round_nearest: 99,135 operations, none different.
The package takes its guard bits and its denormal numbers as parameters. On 1,000 calls per cell in float16, float32 and float64:
setting                        calls       patched right, shipped wrong     neither right     shipped right, patched wrong
3 guard bits, the default      258,075     28,103                           0                 0
4 guard bits                   258,075     27,758                           0                 0
2 guard bits                   258,075     28,760                           112               0
1 guard bit                    258,075     30,212                           2,194             0
denormalize false              255,606     27,745                           0                 0
With two guard bits 112 results of add and subtract are not the rounded ones, and with one guard bit 2,194 of add, subtract and multiply: too few bits to round every result, and both packages return the same bits on those lines. With no guard bits nothing is rounded: the patched sqrt, mac and reciprocal then return the exact result cut off toward zero on every line, where the shipped ones do on 10,207 of 11,141, 3,902 of 9,750 and 10,749 of 11,102. With denormalize false the comparison is with that setting's own numbers, where an exponent field of zero is one more binade of numbers with a leading one.
ieee.fixed_pkg comes from the same source, ieee/fixed_generic_pkg-body.vhdl, and NVC ships that file byte for byte. Every operand of six pairs of small index ranges, unsigned and signed, went through every arithmetic operation: 751,418 calls. Addition, subtraction, multiplication, rem, mod, the comparisons, add_carry, resize in both rounding and both overflow styles, to_integer, scalb, unary minus and abs are right on every one. Division is not.
                                              ufixed               sfixed
/                                             1,073 of 19,984      952 of 19,984
divide, fixed_round, 1 guard bit              4,632                4,376
divide, fixed_round, 2 guard bits             2,433                2,370
divide, fixed_round, 3 guard bits             1,073                952
divide, fixed_round, 4 guard bits             487                  402
divide, fixed_truncate, 3 guard bits          0                    651
divide, no guard bits                         0                    7,785
0.125 / 1.875 in ufixed (3 downto -3) returns 0.0625. The quotient is 0.0667, and the nearest number the result can hold is 0.0703.
The quotient is an integer division carried to the guard bits (lines 604 and 646), and the rounding sees those bits and not the remainder of the division. Guard bits of 100 with a remainder behind them are above the halfway point, and they are rounded as a tie, to even. That is about one quotient in twenty with the default three guard bits. A signed division cuts toward zero and resize then cuts toward minus infinity, so with fixed_truncate a negative quotient goes one way or the other depending on where its guard bits end.
fixed_generic_pkg-body.patch gives both divide functions one more bit below the guard bits, set when the division leaves a remainder, and takes a negative signed quotient that is not exact down to the next lower number before resize rounds or cuts it. reciprocal calls divide. With it nothing is wrong in the 751,418 calls, and GHDL and NVC write the same result file with either package.
sh run_fixed.sh ghdl /path/to/ieee    # 41026 wrong in 751418 operations, then 0 wrong in 751418 operations
sh run_fixed.sh nvc  /path/to/ieee
tb_fixed.vhd is the testbench and fixed_exact.py the reference.
GHDL or NVC, Python 3.9 or later, and the three files float_generic_pkg.vhdl, float_generic_pkg-body.vhdl and float_pkg.vhdl from the directory ieee of the IEEE repository or from lib/ghdl/src/ieee2008 of a GHDL installation.
sh run.sh ghdl /path/to/ieee 100      # plain.vhd, then 100 vectors per operation, format and style: half a minute
sh run.sh nvc  /path/to/ieee 10000    # the tables above
With 100 the last lines read 3696 wrong and 8 stops in 34440 operations for the package as shipped and 0 wrong and 0 stops in 34440 operations for the patched one, in both simulators. NVC is the faster of the two on the large runs: the shipped sqrt and divide work in fixed point and are slow in float128.
run.sh compiles the patched body into a library of its own, patched, beside the declaration it belongs to, and runs each testbench twice: against the package the simulator ships and against that library. tb_float_pkg.vhd reads one operation per line and writes the result; exact.py run restarts it behind a line that stops the simulator.
python3 exact.py gen 2026 10000 5,10 8,23 11,52 15,112 > vectors.txt     # the vectors of the tables above
python3 exact.py all 3,3 > vectors.txt                                   # every operand of the 7-bit format
python3 exact.py check vectors.txt results.txt
To use the patched package in a design, compile float_generic_pkg.vhdl and the patched body into a library and instantiate float_pkg from it, as run.sh does.
To the IEEE P1076 working group on 13 November 2026 as VHDL-Issues#388 and by mail, and as ghdl/ghdl#3486 and nickg/nvc#1758. The VHDL-93 version of the package, float_pkg_c.vhdl in FPHDL/fphdl, carries the same lines: FPHDL/fphdl#4.
